

There are many important applications of complex analysis that highlight its pivotal place in the solution of real-world problems. The ones that we present in this section deal with a fundamental equation of applied mathematics, known as Laplace's equation. This equation models important phenomena in engineering and physics, such as steady-state temperature distributions, electrostatic potentials, and fluid flow, to name just a few. For clarity's sake, we will base our presentation around steady-state temperature problems. The solutions that we present use almost all the material that we have covered thus far, and more important, they stress the need for further development of the theory.



# 2.5.1 Laplace's Equation and Harmonic Functions



> [!review]
> 1. A homogeneous plate with insulated lateral surfaces has its boundary held at a fixed temperature distribution $b(x, y)$ that does not vary in time. What happens to the temperature inside the plate over time, and what partial differential equation is satisfied by this long-term temperature distribution?
> 2. Define the Laplacian $\Delta u$ of a twice-differentiable real-valued function $u(x, y)$.
> 3. What does the Laplacian of a function $u$ measure about $u$ 's behavior near a point, and what does this imply about a function whose Laplacian is zero?
> 4. What does it mean for a real-valued function $u$ to be harmonic on a region $\Omega$ in the plane?
> 5. What makes an equation a partial differential equation?
> 6. What is a boundary value problem, and what special case of a boundary value problem is known as a Dirichlet problem?





##### problem 1

If the boundary of a homogeneous plate is held at a fixed temperature distribution $b(x,y)$ that does not change with time, then the temperature inside the plate evolves with time until it eventually settles into an equilibrium distribution. After enough time has passed, the temperature no longer changes, so the plate reaches a steady-state temperature distribution.

If we denote this long-term temperature by $u(x,y)$, then thermodynamics tells us that $u$ satisfies the two-dimensional Laplace equation in the interior of the plate:

$$
\Delta u=\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial y^2}=0.
$$

Thus the long-term temperature distribution is a harmonic function whose boundary values are prescribed by the given boundary temperature function $b(x,y)$.

##### problem 2

If $u(x,y)$ is a twice-differentiable real-valued function, then its Laplacian is defined by

$$
\Delta u=\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial y^2}.
$$

So the Laplacian is the sum of the second partial derivative of $u$ with respect to $x$ and the second partial derivative of $u$ with respect to $y$.

##### problem 3

The Laplacian measures how the value of a function at a point compares with the average value of the function near that point. In the language of the text, it measures the difference between the value of the function at a point and the average value of the function in a neighborhood of that point.

This gives an important geometric interpretation. If the Laplacian is large in magnitude, then the function is varying sharply near that point. If the Laplacian is small, then the function varies more gently and regularly there.

In particular, if $\Delta u=0$, then the function has zero Laplacian, so it behaves in an especially regular way. Such a function does not exhibit abrupt local variation of the kind measured by the Laplacian. This is why functions satisfying Laplace's equation are called harmonic.

##### problem 4

A real-valued function $u$ is said to be harmonic on a region $\Omega$ if it satisfies two conditions throughout $\Omega$.

First, $u$ must satisfy Laplace's equation:

$$
\Delta u=0.
$$

Second, $u$ must have continuous first and second partial derivatives on $\Omega$.

Therefore, saying that $u$ is harmonic on $\Omega$ means that $u$ is a sufficiently smooth real-valued solution of Laplace's equation on that region.

##### problem 5

An equation is called a partial differential equation when it involves partial derivatives of an unknown function of several variables. The word "partial" indicates that the derivatives are taken with respect to individual variables while the others are held fixed.

For example, Laplace's equation

$$
\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial y^2}=0
$$

is a partial differential equation because the unknown function $u(x,y)$ depends on more than one variable and the equation involves its partial derivatives.

##### problem 6

A boundary value problem is a problem in which one seeks a function satisfying a differential equation inside a region together with prescribed conditions on the boundary of that region.

In the present setting, one wants to determine a function $u(x,y)$ inside a region $\Omega$ so that $u$ satisfies Laplace's equation in $\Omega$ and also satisfies a boundary condition on $\partial\Omega$, such as

$$
u(x,y)=b(x,y)
\qquad\text{on the boundary of } \Omega.
$$

The special case in which the boundary values themselves are specified is called a Dirichlet problem. Thus, for Laplace's equation, a Dirichlet problem asks for a harmonic function inside the region whose values on the boundary are already prescribed.



+++++



> [!exercise]
> Verify that constants and the functions $u(x, y)=x, u(x, y)=y, u(x, y)=x y$ are each harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.


##### solution

To verify that each function is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$, we compute its Laplacian

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}
$$

and check that it is equal to $0$ everywhere.

First consider a constant function, say $u(x,y)=c$, where $c$ is a real constant. Then

$$
u_x=0,
\qquad
u_y=0.
$$

Differentiating once more gives

$$
u_{xx}=0,
\qquad
u_{yy}=0.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0+0=0.
$$

So every constant function is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

Next consider $u(x,y)=x$. Then

$$
u_x=1,
\qquad
u_y=0.
$$

Differentiating again, we obtain

$$
u_{xx}=0,
\qquad
u_{yy}=0.
$$

Hence

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0+0=0.
$$

Therefore $u(x,y)=x$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

Now consider $u(x,y)=y$. Then

$$
u_x=0,
\qquad
u_y=1.
$$

Differentiating again gives

$$
u_{xx}=0,
\qquad
u_{yy}=0.
$$

So

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0+0=0.
$$

Therefore $u(x,y)=y$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

Finally consider $u(x,y)=xy$. Then

$$
u_x=y,
\qquad
u_y=x.
$$

Differentiating once more, we get

$$
u_{xx}=0,
\qquad
u_{yy}=0.
$$

Thus

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0+0=0.
$$

Therefore $u(x,y)=xy$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

We conclude that constants and the functions $x$, $y$, and $xy$ are all harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.


++++




Consider a two-dimensional plate of homogeneous material, with instlated lateral surfaces. We represent this plate by a region $\Omega$ in the complex plane (see _Figure 1_). Suppose that the temperature of the points on the boundary of the plate is given by a function of position $b(x, y)$ that does not change with time. It is a fact from thermodynamics that the temperature inside the plate will eventually reach and remain at an equilibrium


> [!figure] Figure 1
> 
> ![](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-37_459_508_201_110.jpg)
> Figure 1 The steady-state temperature distribution satisfies Laplace's equation.



The Laplacian is named after the great French mathematician and physicist PierreSimon de Laplace (17491827). The Laplacian appeared for the first time in a memoir of Laplace in 1784, in which he completely determined the attraction of a spheroid on the points outside it. The Laplacian of a function measures the difference between the value of the function at a point and the average value of the function in a neighborhood of that point. Thus a function that does not vary abruptly has a very small Laplacian. Harmonic functions have a zero Laplacian; they vary in a very regular way. Examples of such functions include the temperature distribution in a plate, the potential of the attractive force due to a sphere, the function that gives the brightness of colors in an image.
distribution, known as the steady-state distribution. For ( $x, y$ ) in $\Omega$, let $u(x, y)$ denote this steady-state temperature distribution. It is also a fact from thermodynamics that $u(x, y)$ satisfies the (two-dimensional) Laplace's equation

$$
\Delta u=\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial y^{2}}=0
$$

Here $\Delta u$ is the Laplacian of $u$, which by definition is $\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial y^{2}}$. Since Laplace's equation involves partial derivatives, it is called a partial differential equation. Any real-valued function that satisfies Laplace's equation on a region $\Omega$ and has continuous first and second partial derivatives is called harmonic on $\Omega$. Constant functions are clearly harmonic on the entire plane, and so are the functions $u(x, y)=x, u(x, y)=y, u(x, y)=x y$. Less obvious examples of harmonic functions will be derived from Theorem 1 later in this section.

To determine the steady-state temperature inside the plate, we must solve Laplace's equation inside of $\Omega$ subject to the condition $u(x, y)= b(x, y)$ on the boundary of $\Omega$, known as a boundary condition. A problem consisting of a partial differential equation along with specified boundary conditions is known as a boundary value problem. The special case involving Laplace's equation with specified boundary values is known as a Dirichlet problem. Solving Dirichlet problems is of paramount importance in applied mathematics, engineering, and physics. Many methods have been developed. The ones that we will present in this section provide a beautiful application of complex analysis.




# 2.5.2 Connection with Analytic Functions



> [!review]
> How can analytic functions be used to produce harmonic functions? Prove your answer.



##### solution

Analytic functions provide a systematic way to produce harmonic functions: if $f=u+i v$ is analytic on an open set $S$, then both its real part $u(x,y)$ and its imaginary part $v(x,y)$ are harmonic on $S$.

The reason is that analyticity gives the Cauchy-Riemann equations

$$
u_x=v_y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

To show that $u$ is harmonic, we compute its Laplacian. Differentiate the first Cauchy-Riemann equation with respect to $x$:

$$
u_{xx}=v_{yx}.
$$

Differentiate the second Cauchy-Riemann equation with respect to $y$:

$$
u_{yy}=-v_{xy}.
$$

Now add these two equations:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}+u_{yy}
&=v_{yx}+(-v_{xy})\\
&=v_{yx}-v_{xy}.
\end{aligned}
$$

If the relevant second partial derivatives are continuous, then mixed partial derivatives may be interchanged, so $v_{yx}=v_{xy}$. Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}+u_{yy}
&=v_{xy}-v_{xy}\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0,
$$

so $u$ is harmonic on $S$.

The same argument works for $v$. Differentiate $u_x=v_y$ with respect to $y$:

$$
u_{xy}=v_{yy}.
$$

Differentiate $u_y=-v_x$ with respect to $x$:

$$
u_{yx}=-v_{xx}.
$$

Since $u_{xy}=u_{yx}$ when the second partial derivatives are continuous, we obtain

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_{yy}
&=u_{xy}\\
&=u_{yx}\\
&=-v_{xx}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_{xx}+v_{yy}
&=v_{xx}+(-v_{xx})\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\Delta v=v_{xx}+v_{yy}=0,
$$

so $v$ is also harmonic on $S$.

We conclude that every analytic function produces harmonic functions simply by taking its real part or its imaginary part. This is the content of the theorem stated in the section: if $f=u+i v$ is analytic on an open set $S$, then both $u$ and $v$ are harmonic on $S$.



+++++


Let $f=u+i v$ be an analytic function. We know that $u$ and $v$ satisfy the Cauchy-Riemann equations (Theorem 1, Section 2.4),

$$
\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}=\frac{\partial v}{\partial y} \quad \text { and } \quad \frac{\partial u}{\partial y}=-\frac{\partial v}{\partial x} .
$$

Let us suppose for a moment that we can differentiate $u$ and $v$ twice and interchange the order of partial derivatives. Then

$$
\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial x^{2}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{\partial v}{\partial y}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(-\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}\right)=-\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial y^{2}},
$$

and hence $\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial y^{2}}=0$. In other words, $u$ satisfies Laplace's equation. Reasoning in a similar way, we can show that $v$ also satisfies Laplace's equation.

Recall from calculus that in order to justify the interchange of partial derivatives as we needed to do above, it is enough to know that all first and


second partial derivatives are continuous. One of the major results that we will develop in the following chapter guarantees that the real and imaginary parts of an analytic function have continuous partial derivatives of all orders. Thus, the steps that we used above are justified and $u$ and $v$ are harmonic.


> [!theorem] Theorem 1: Analytic and Harmonic Functions
> Suppose that $f=u+i v$ is analytic on an open set $S$. Then its real and inaginary parts $u(x, y)$ and $v(x, y)$ are harmonic on $S$.


What this result effectively does for us is provide us with many examples of harmonic functions; simply take the real or imaginary part of any analytic function.



> [!exercise] Exercise 1: Harmonic functions
> Show that the following are harmonic functions in the stated region.
> (a) $u(x, y)=x^{2}-y^{2}$ on $\mathbb{C}$.
> (b) $u(x, y)=e^{x} \sin y$ on $\mathbb{C}$.
> (c) $u(x, y)=\operatorname{Arg} z(z=(x, y))$ on the region $\Omega=\mathbb{C} \backslash(-\infty, 0]$.
> (d) $u(x, y)=\ln |z|=\ln \sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}$ on the region $\mathbb{C} \backslash\{0\}$.



##### solution

We verify in each case that the function has continuous first and second partial derivatives on the stated region and that its Laplacian is zero there.

##### problem (a)

Let

$$
u(x,y)=x^2-y^2.
$$

Compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
u_x=2x,
\qquad
u_y=-2y.
$$

Now compute the second partial derivatives:

$$
u_{xx}=2,
\qquad
u_{yy}=-2.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=2+(-2)=0.
$$

Since these derivatives exist and are continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{C}$, the function $u(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}$.

##### problem (b)

Let

$$
u(x,y)=e^x\sin y.
$$

Compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
u_x=e^x\sin y,
\qquad
u_y=e^x\cos y.
$$

Now compute the second partial derivatives:

$$
u_{xx}=e^x\sin y,
\qquad
u_{yy}=-e^x\sin y.
$$

Hence

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=e^x\sin y+(-e^x\sin y)=0.
$$

These derivatives are continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{C}$, so $u(x,y)=e^x\sin y$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}$.

##### problem (c)

Let

$$
u(x,y)=\operatorname{Arg} z.
$$

On the region

$$
\Omega=\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0],
$$

the principal argument is single-valued. Its derivatives are the same as those obtained by differentiating the local expression

$$
u(x,y)=\arctan\!\left(\frac{y}{x}\right),
$$

which is valid locally away from the branch cut. We now compute its derivatives for $(x,y)\neq(0,0)$.

Set

$$
t=\frac{y}{x}.
$$

Then

$$
u(x,y)=\arctan(t).
$$

Differentiate with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{1}{1+t^2}\,t_x\\
&=\frac{1}{1+\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^2}\left(-\frac{y}{x^2}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{\frac{x^2+y^2}{x^2}}\left(-\frac{y}{x^2}\right)\\
&=\frac{x^2}{x^2+y^2}\left(-\frac{y}{x^2}\right)\\
&=-\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{1}{1+t^2}\,t_y\\
&=\frac{1}{1+\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^2}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{\frac{x^2+y^2}{x^2}}\cdot\frac{1}{x}\\
&=\frac{x^2}{x^2+y^2}\cdot\frac{1}{x}\\
&=\frac{x}{x^2+y^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now compute the second partial derivatives. Since

$$
u_x=-y(x^2+y^2)^{-1},
$$

we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}
&=-y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2+y^2)^{-1}\\
&=-y\left(-(x^2+y^2)^{-2}(2x)\right)\\
&=\frac{2xy}{(x^2+y^2)^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Since

$$
u_y=x(x^2+y^2)^{-1},
$$

we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{yy}
&=x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2+y^2)^{-1}\\
&=x\left(-(x^2+y^2)^{-2}(2y)\right)\\
&=-\frac{2xy}{(x^2+y^2)^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}
=\frac{2xy}{(x^2+y^2)^2}-\frac{2xy}{(x^2+y^2)^2}
=0.
$$

The function and its derivatives are well defined and continuous on $\Omega=\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]$, so $\operatorname{Arg} z$ is harmonic on that region.

##### problem (d)

Let

$$
u(x,y)=\ln|z|=\ln\sqrt{x^2+y^2}.
$$

Since

$$
\ln\sqrt{x^2+y^2}
=\ln\bigl((x^2+y^2)^{1/2}\bigr)
=\frac12\ln(x^2+y^2),
$$

we may write

$$
u(x,y)=\frac12\ln(x^2+y^2)
$$

for $(x,y)\neq(0,0)$.

Now compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac12\cdot\frac{1}{x^2+y^2}\cdot 2x\\
&=\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac12\cdot\frac{1}{x^2+y^2}\cdot 2y\\
&=\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now compute the second partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(x(x^2+y^2)^{-1}\right)\\
&=(x^2+y^2)^{-1}+x\left(-(x^2+y^2)^{-2}(2x)\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{x^2+y^2}-\frac{2x^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}\\
&=\frac{x^2+y^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}-\frac{2x^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}\\
&=\frac{y^2-x^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2},
\end{aligned}
$$

and similarly

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(y(x^2+y^2)^{-1}\right)\\
&=(x^2+y^2)^{-1}+y\left(-(x^2+y^2)^{-2}(2y)\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{x^2+y^2}-\frac{2y^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}\\
&=\frac{x^2+y^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}-\frac{2y^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}\\
&=\frac{x^2-y^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta u
&=u_{xx}+u_{yy}\\
&=\frac{y^2-x^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}+\frac{x^2-y^2}{(x^2+y^2)^2}\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus $\ln|z|$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}$.



++++




> [!review]
> 1. Under what conditions is a linear combination $a u+b v$ of two functions $u, v$ harmonic on an open set $S$ ? Prove your answer.
> 2. What can be said about the pointwise product $u v$ of two harmonic functions $u, v$ on an open set $S$ ? Justify your answer.



##### problem 1

If $u$ and $v$ are harmonic on an open set $S$, and if $a$ and $b$ are real constants, then the linear combination

$$
a u+b v
$$

is also harmonic on $S$.

The reason is straightforward. Since $u$ and $v$ are harmonic on $S$, they have continuous first and second partial derivatives on $S$, and they satisfy

$$
\Delta u=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Delta v=0
$$

throughout $S$.

Now let

$$
\phi(x,y)=a u(x,y)+b v(x,y).
$$

Because differentiation is linear, the second partial derivatives of $\phi$ are

$$
\phi_{xx}=a u_{xx}+b v_{xx},
\qquad
\phi_{yy}=a u_{yy}+b v_{yy}.
$$

Therefore its Laplacian is

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta \phi
&=\phi_{xx}+\phi_{yy}\\
&=(a u_{xx}+b v_{xx})+(a u_{yy}+b v_{yy})\\
&=a(u_{xx}+u_{yy})+b(v_{xx}+v_{yy})\\
&=a\,\Delta u+b\,\Delta v\\
&=a\cdot 0+b\cdot 0\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $\phi$ also has continuous first and second partial derivatives on $S$, it follows that $\phi$ is harmonic on $S$.

So the condition is simply this: both $u$ and $v$ must already be harmonic on the same open set $S$, and $a,b$ must be real constants. Under those conditions, every linear combination $a u+b v$ is harmonic on $S$.

##### problem 2

In general, the pointwise product $u v$ of two harmonic functions need not be harmonic.

There is no product rule for harmonicity analogous to the linearity property above. In fact, even if both $u$ and $v$ satisfy Laplace's equation, their product may fail to do so.

To see this concretely, take

$$
u(x,y)=x
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v(x,y)=x.
$$

Each of these functions is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$, because

$$
u_{xx}=0,\qquad u_{yy}=0,
$$

so

$$
\Delta u=0,
$$

and the same calculation holds for $v$.

But their product is

$$
u(x,y)v(x,y)=x\cdot x=x^2.
$$

Now compute the Laplacian of $x^2$:

$$
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2)=2x,
\qquad
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2)=0,
$$

so

$$
\frac{\partial^2}{\partial x^2}(x^2)=2,
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2}{\partial y^2}(x^2)=0.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta(x^2)=2+0=2\neq 0.
$$

So $x^2$ is not harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

This example shows that the product of two harmonic functions is not, in general, harmonic. It may happen in special cases that a product is harmonic, but there is no general theorem guaranteeing that harmonicity is preserved under multiplication.


+++++


> [!exercise]
> 1. Show that $\phi(x, y)=2\left(x^2-y^2\right)+7$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.
> 2. Show that $\phi(x, y)=(a x+b)(c y+d)$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$ for any real constants $a, b, c, d$ .
> 3. Show that $u(x, y)=x^2$ is not harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.


##### solution

##### problem 1

Let

$$
\phi(x,y)=2(x^2-y^2)+7.
$$

First expand the expression:

$$
\phi(x,y)=2x^2-2y^2+7.
$$

Now compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
\phi_x=4x,
\qquad
\phi_y=-4y.
$$

Differentiate once more:

$$
\phi_{xx}=4,
\qquad
\phi_{yy}=-4.
$$

Therefore the Laplacian is

$$
\Delta \phi=\phi_{xx}+\phi_{yy}=4+(-4)=0.
$$

Since the relevant partial derivatives are continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{R}^2$, the function $\phi(x,y)=2(x^2-y^2)+7$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.

##### problem 2

Let

$$
\phi(x,y)=(ax+b)(cy+d),
$$

where $a,b,c,d$ are real constants.

First expand:

$$
\phi(x,y)=ac\,xy+ad\,x+bc\,y+bd.
$$

Now compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
\phi_x=ac\,y+ad,
\qquad
\phi_y=ac\,x+bc.
$$

Differentiate again:

$$
\phi_{xx}=0,
\qquad
\phi_{yy}=0.
$$

Hence

$$
\Delta \phi=\phi_{xx}+\phi_{yy}=0+0=0.
$$

Therefore $\phi(x,y)=(ax+b)(cy+d)$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$ for all real constants $a,b,c,d$.

##### problem 3

Let

$$
u(x,y)=x^2.
$$

Compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
u_x=2x,
\qquad
u_y=0.
$$

Differentiate again:

$$
u_{xx}=2,
\qquad
u_{yy}=0.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=2+0=2.
$$

Since $\Delta u\neq 0$, the function $u(x,y)=x^2$ does not satisfy Laplace's equation. Hence it is not harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$.


++++






> [!proposition] Proposition 1: Linearity
> Suppose that $u$ and $v$ are harmonic on an open set $S$, and $a, b$ are resed constants. Then $a u(x, y)+b v(x, y)$ is harmonic on $S$.



For example, $\phi(x, y)=2\left(x^{2}-y^{2}\right)+7$ is harmonic, being of the form $\phi(x, y)=2 u(x, y)+7$ where $u$ is the harmonic function of Example 1(a). Also the function $\phi(x, y)=(a x+b)(c y+d)$, where $a, b, c, d$ are real constants, is harmonic, being a linear combination of the harmonic functions $x, y, x y$, and constants.

You should be cautioned that the product of two harmonic functions need not be harmonic. For example, $u(x, y)=x$ is harmonic, but $(u(x, y))^{2}=x^{2}$ is not.





# 2.5.3 Harmonic Conjugates


> [!review]
> Given a harmonic function $u$ on a region $\Omega$, what additional function must be supplied to produce an analytic function $f$ on $\Omega$ with $\operatorname{Re}(f)=u$, and what properties must it satisfy? Is the required function always guaranteed to exist on $\Omega$ ?



##### solution

To produce an analytic function $f$ on $\Omega$ with real part

$$
\operatorname{Re}(f)=u,
$$

one must supply another real-valued function $v$ on $\Omega$ such that

$$
f=u+i v
$$

is analytic in $\Omega$.

Such a function $v$ is called a harmonic conjugate of $u$. Its role is not merely to fill in the imaginary part formally; it must be chosen so that the resulting complex-valued function $f$ actually satisfies the conditions for analyticity. In particular, if $f=u+i v$ is analytic, then $u$ and $v$ must satisfy the Cauchy-Riemann equations on $\Omega$:

$$
u_x=v_y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Thus the additional function must be a function $v$ whose partial derivatives are related to those of $u$ in exactly this way. When such a function exists, $v$ is called a harmonic conjugate of $u$, and the function $f=u+i v$ is analytic on $\Omega$.

Moreover, because the real and imaginary parts of an analytic function are harmonic, a harmonic conjugate is itself harmonic. So the desired function is not just any auxiliary function; it must be another harmonic function that pairs with $u$ through the Cauchy-Riemann equations.

However, such a function is not always guaranteed to exist on a given region $\Omega$. The existence of a harmonic conjugate depends on the nature of the region. The section explicitly warns that this is a geometric issue tied to the domain.

For example, the function

$$
u(z)=\ln|z|
$$

is harmonic on $\Omega=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}$, but it does not have a harmonic conjugate on that entire region. On the other hand, if we restrict to the slit plane

$$
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0],
$$

then $\ln|z|$ does have a harmonic conjugate there, namely $\operatorname{Arg} z$.

So the answer is this: to obtain an analytic function with real part $u$, one must find a harmonic conjugate $v$ such that $f=u+i v$ satisfies the Cauchy-Riemann equations and is analytic on $\Omega$. But the existence of such a $v$ is not automatic on every region; it depends on the domain under consideration.


+++++


Reading Theorem 1, it is natural to ask the following question: Given a harmonic function $u$ in a region $\Omega$, can we find another harmonic function $v$ in $\Omega$ such that $f=u+i v$ is analytic in $\Omega$ ? Such a function $v$ is called a harmonic conjugate of $u$. Conditions for the existence of a harmonic conjugate will be established in Chapter 3. For now, just keep in mind that the existence of a harmonic conjugate depends on the nature of the region under consideration. For example, the function $\ln |z|$ is harmonic in $\Omega= \mathbb{C} \backslash\{0\}$ (Example 1(d)); but $\ln |z|$ has no harmonic conjugate in that region (Exercise 33). It does, however, have a harmonic conjugate in $\mathbb{C} \backslash(-\infty, 0]$, namely $\operatorname{Arg} z$. As our next example shows, by integrating the CauchyRiemann equations, we can always find the harmonic conjugate in a region such as the entire complex plane, a disk, or a rectangle. (More generally, we will show in Chapter 3 that we can find a harmonic conjugate if the region is simply connected. This more restrictive concept of connectedness is extremely important and will be at the heart of Chapter 3.)



> [!exercise] Exercise 2: Finding harmonic conjugates
> Show that $u(x, y)=x^{2}-y^{2}+x$ is harmonic in the entire plane and find a harmonic conjugate.



##### solution

We must do two things: show that

$$
u(x,y)=x^2-y^2+x
$$

is harmonic on the entire plane, and then find a function $v(x,y)$ such that

$$
f(z)=u(x,y)+i v(x,y)
$$

is analytic.

First compute the partial derivatives of $u$:

$$
u_x=2x+1,
\qquad
u_y=-2y.
$$

Differentiate once more:

$$
u_{xx}=2,
\qquad
u_{yy}=-2.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=2+(-2)=0.
$$

Since these partial derivatives are continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{R}^2$, the function $u$ is harmonic on the entire plane.

Now we find a harmonic conjugate $v$. If

$$
f=u+i v
$$

is analytic, then the Cauchy-Riemann equations must hold:

$$
u_x=v_y,
\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Substitute the derivatives of $u$:

$$
v_y=2x+1,
\qquad
-v_x=-2y.
$$

So

$$
v_y=2x+1,
\qquad
v_x=2y.
$$

We now integrate $v_x=2y$ with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=\int 2y\,dx\\
&=2xy+\phi(y),
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\phi(y)$ is an arbitrary function of $y$, because differentiation with respect to $x$ treats $y$ as a constant.

Next differentiate this expression with respect to $y$:

$$
v_y=2x+\phi'(y).
$$

But from the Cauchy-Riemann equations we also need

$$
v_y=2x+1.
$$

Therefore

$$
2x+\phi'(y)=2x+1,
$$

so

$$
\phi'(y)=1.
$$

Integrating with respect to $y$, we obtain

$$
\phi(y)=y+C,
$$

where $C$ is a real constant.

Substitute this back into the formula for $v$:

$$
v(x,y)=2xy+y+C.
$$

Thus a harmonic conjugate of $u$ is

$$
\boxed{v(x,y)=2xy+y}
$$

up to an additive real constant.

Accordingly, one analytic function having real part $u$ is

$$
f(z)=x^2-y^2+x+i(2xy+y).
$$

Since

$$
z^2=(x+iy)^2=x^2-y^2+2ixy
$$

and

$$
z=x+iy,
$$

we can also write this analytic function more compactly as

$$
f(z)=z^2+z+iC.
$$

So the answer is that $u(x,y)=x^2-y^2+x$ is harmonic on the whole plane, and one of its harmonic conjugates is $v(x,y)=2xy+y$.


++++


> [!review]
> Given that $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of a harmonic function $u$, how can a harmonic conjugate of $v$ be expressed in terms of $u$ ? Prove your answer.



##### solution

If $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, then a harmonic conjugate of $v$ is $-u$.

To prove this, start from the definition. Saying that $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$ means that

$$
f=u+i v
$$

is analytic. Therefore the Cauchy-Riemann equations hold:

$$
u_x=v_y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Now consider the function

$$
g=v+i(-u)=v-i u.
$$

If we can show that $g$ is analytic, then by definition $-u$ will be a harmonic conjugate of $v$.

For the real part $v$ and the imaginary part $-u$, the Cauchy-Riemann equations would be

$$
v_x=(-u)_y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v_y=-(-u)_x.
$$

Compute the right-hand sides:

$$
(-u)_y=-u_y,
\qquad
-(-u)_x=u_x.
$$

So the required equations become

$$
v_x=-u_y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v_y=u_x.
$$

But these are exactly the same as the original Cauchy-Riemann equations written in rearranged form, since

$$
u_y=-v_x
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
v_x=-u_y,
$$

and

$$
u_x=v_y
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
v_y=u_x.
$$

Therefore the Cauchy-Riemann equations hold for $g=v+i(-u)$, so $g$ is analytic.

Hence $-u$ is a harmonic conjugate of $v$.

So the answer is:

$$
\boxed{\text{a harmonic conjugate of }v\text{ is }-u.}
$$


+++++


So, from Example 2, a harmonic conjugate of $u(x, y)=x^{2}-y^{2}+x$ is $v(x, y)=2 x y+y$. What is a harmonic conjugate of $v(x, y)=2 x y+y$ ? Surely it is related to $u$. Indeed, you can check that a conjugate of $v$ is $-u(x, y)=-x^{2}+y^{2}-x$. More generally, we have the following useful result.

> [!proposition] Proposition 2
> Suppose that $u$ is harmonic and that $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$. Then $-u$ is a harmonic conjugate of $v$.


**Proof** 






> [!review]
> 1. If $v_1$ and $v_2$ are both harmonic conjugates of the same harmonic function $u$ on a region $\Omega$, how must $v_1$ and $v_2$ be related? Prove your answer.
> 2. If $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of a harmonic function $u$ on a region $\Omega$, determine whether $v$ is also a harmonic conjugate of $u+c$ for every real constant $c$. Prove your answer.
> 3. What does it mean for two curves in the plane to be orthogonal at a point of intersection, and what does it mean for two families of curves to be orthogonal?
> 4. What is the geometric relationship between the level curves of a harmonic function $u$ and those of its harmonic conjugate $v$ on a region $\Omega$ ? Prove your answer.



##### problem 1

If $v_1$ and $v_2$ are both harmonic conjugates of the same harmonic function $u$ on a region $\Omega$, then $v_1$ and $v_2$ must differ by a real constant. In other words, there exists a real constant $c$ such that

$$
v_1=v_2+c
$$

throughout $\Omega$.

To prove this, note that since both $v_1$ and $v_2$ are harmonic conjugates of $u$, the functions

$$
f_1=u+i v_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
f_2=u+i v_2
$$

are analytic on $\Omega$. Hence both pairs $(u,v_1)$ and $(u,v_2)$ satisfy the Cauchy-Riemann equations:

$$
u_x=(v_1)_y,
\qquad
u_y=-(v_1)_x,
$$

and

$$
u_x=(v_2)_y,
\qquad
u_y=-(v_2)_x.
$$

Subtract the corresponding equations. From the first pair we get

$$
(v_1)_y-(v_2)_y=0,
$$

so

$$
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(v_1-v_2)=0.
$$

From the second pair we get

$$
(v_1)_x-(v_2)_x=0,
$$

so

$$
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(v_1-v_2)=0.
$$

Thus both partial derivatives of $v_1-v_2$ are zero on $\Omega$. Therefore $v_1-v_2$ is constant on the region $\Omega$, and hence

$$
v_1=v_2+c
$$

for some real constant $c$.

##### problem 2

Yes. If $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$ on $\Omega$, then $v$ is also a harmonic conjugate of $u+c$ for every real constant $c$.

Indeed, if $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, then

$$
f=u+i v
$$

is analytic on $\Omega$, so the Cauchy-Riemann equations hold:

$$
u_x=v_y,
\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Now let

$$
\tilde u=u+c,
$$

where $c$ is real and constant. Then

$$
\tilde u_x=u_x,
\qquad
\tilde u_y=u_y,
$$

because the derivatives of a constant are zero.

Therefore

$$
\tilde u_x=u_x=v_y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\tilde u_y=u_y=-v_x.
$$

So the pair $(\tilde u,v)$ also satisfies the Cauchy-Riemann equations. Hence

$$
(u+c)+i v
$$

is analytic on $\Omega$, which means that $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u+c$.

##### problem 3

Two curves in the plane are said to be orthogonal at a point of intersection if their tangent lines at that point are perpendicular. Equivalently, they intersect at a right angle there.

If the tangent lines have slopes $m_1$ and $m_2$, and neither slope is zero or undefined in a problematic way, then orthogonality is expressed by the relation

$$
m_1m_2=-1.
$$

Two families of curves are said to be orthogonal if every curve from one family intersects every curve from the other family at right angles whenever they meet.

##### problem 4

The level curves of a harmonic function $u$ and those of its harmonic conjugate $v$ are orthogonal to each other at every point where

$$
f'(z)\neq 0,
$$

where

$$
f=u+i v.
$$

To prove this, consider first a level curve of $u$, given implicitly by

$$
u(x,y)=C_1,
$$

where $C_1$ is constant. Differentiate implicitly with respect to $x$:

$$
u_x\frac{dx}{dx}+u_y\frac{dy}{dx}=0.
$$

Since $dx/dx=1$, this becomes

$$
u_x+u_y\frac{dy}{dx}=0.
$$

Assuming $u_y\neq 0$, we solve for the slope:

$$
\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{u_x}{u_y}.
$$

So the slope of the tangent line to a level curve of $u$ is

$$
m_u=-\frac{u_x}{u_y}.
$$

Now consider a level curve of $v$, given by

$$
v(x,y)=C_2.
$$

Differentiate implicitly with respect to $x$:

$$
v_x\frac{dx}{dx}+v_y\frac{dy}{dx}=0,
$$

so

$$
v_x+v_y\frac{dy}{dx}=0.
$$

Assuming $v_y\neq 0$, the slope is

$$
\frac{dy}{dx}=-\frac{v_x}{v_y}.
$$

Thus the slope of the tangent line to a level curve of $v$ is

$$
m_v=-\frac{v_x}{v_y}.
$$

Now use the Cauchy-Riemann equations:

$$
v_x=-u_y,
\qquad
v_y=u_x.
$$

Substitute these into the formula for $m_v$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
m_v
&=-\frac{v_x}{v_y}\\
&=-\frac{-u_y}{u_x}\\
&=\frac{u_y}{u_x}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
m_u m_v
&=\left(-\frac{u_x}{u_y}\right)\left(\frac{u_y}{u_x}\right)\\
&=-1.
\end{aligned}
$$

So the tangent lines are perpendicular, and therefore the level curves of $u$ are orthogonal to the level curves of $v$.

This argument is valid at points where the relevant derivatives do not vanish in a way that destroys the tangent-line computation, which is summarized in the theorem by the condition

$$
f'(z)\neq 0.
$$

Hence the geometric relationship is that the two families of level curves,

$$
u(x,y)=C_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v(x,y)=C_2,
$$

form an orthogonal family.



+++++



In Example 2, we found the harmonic conjugate of $u$ up to an arbitrary additive real constant. In fact, the following properties of the harmonic conjugate are not hard to prove (Exercise 18).


> [!proposition] Proposition 3
> Suppose that $u$ is a harmonic function in a region $\Omega$. Then
> (i) if $v_{1}$ and $v_{2}$ are harmonic conjugates of $u$ in $\Omega$, then $v_{1}$ and $v_{2}$ must differ by a real constant.
> (ii) If $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, then $v$ is also a harmonic conjugate of $u+c$ where $c$ is any real constant.


The function $u$ and its conjugate $v$ have a very interesting geometric relationship based on the notion of orthogonal curves.

Suppose that two curves $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ meet at a point $A$. The curves are said to be orthogonal if their respective tangent lines $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ (at $A$ ) are orthogonal (_Figure 2_). 



> [!figure] Figure 2
> 
> 
> ![](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-40_480_528_1485_142.jpg)
> Figure 2 The curves $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ are orthogonal at $A$ if the tangent lines $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ (at $A$ ) are orthogonal.



We also say that $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ intersect at a right angle at $A$. Recall that if $m_{1}$ and $m_{2}$ denote the respective slopes of the tangent lines, and if neither is zero, then $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ are orthogonal if and only if

$$
m_{1} m_{2}=-1
$$

Two families of curves are said to be orthogonal if each curve from one family intersects the curves from the other family at right angles.

Consider the level curves of a harmonic function $u(x, y)$ in a region $\Omega$. These are the curves determined by the implicit relation

$$
u(x, y)=C_{1}
$$

where $C_{1}$ is a constant (in the range of $u$ ). Since $u$ is harmonic, it has continuous partial derivatives, and hence we can use the chain rule, Theorem 4, Section 2.6, to differentiate both sides of (4) with respect to $x$ and get

$$
\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} \frac{d x}{d x}+\frac{\partial u}{\partial y} \frac{d y}{d x}=0
$$

But $d x / d x=1$, so if $\frac{\partial u}{\partial y} \neq 0$, we can solve for $d y / d x$ and get

$$
\frac{d y}{d x}=-\frac{\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}}{\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}}
$$

This gives the slope of the tangent line at a point on a level curve. Now suppose that we can find a harmonic conjugate $v(x, y)$ of $u(x, y)$ in $\Omega$ and let us consider the level curves

$$
v(x, y)=C_{2}
$$

where $C_{2}$ is a constant (in the range of $v$ ). Since $v$ is harmonic, arguing as we did with $u$, we find that the slope of the tangent line at a point on a level curve is

$$
\frac{d y}{d x}=-\frac{\frac{\partial v}{\partial x}}{\frac{\partial v}{\partial y}}=\frac{\frac{\partial u}{\partial y}}{\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}}
$$

since by the Cauchy-Riemann equations, $\partial v / \partial x=-\partial u / \partial y$ and $\partial v / \partial y= \partial u / \partial x$. Comparing (6) and (7), we see that the slopes of the tangent lines satisfy the orthogonality relation (3), and hence the level curves of $u$ are orthogonal to the level curves of $v$. This orthogonality relation also holds when the tangents are horizontal and vertical. We thus have the following result.

> [!theorem] Theorem 2: Orthogonality of Level Curves
> Suppose that $u$ is a harmonic function in a region $\Omega$ and let $v$ be a harmonic conjugate of $u$ in $\Omega$, so that $f=u+i v$ is analytic in $\Omega$. Then, the two families of level curves, $u(x, y)=C_{1}$ and $v(x, y)=C_{2}$, are orthogonal at every point where $f^{\prime}(z) \neq 0$.
> 


As an illustration, we show in _Figure 3(a) and (b)_ the level curves of the harmonic function $u=x^{2}-y^{2}+x$ and its conjugate $v(x, y)=2 x y+y$ (see Exercise 2). The graphs of the two families are superposed in Figure 3(c) to illustrate their orthogonality.

# 2.5.4 Solving and Interpreting Dirichlet Problems


> [!review]
> 1. What characterizes the harmonic functions (on appropriate subdomains of $\mathbb{C}$ ) that are constant on rays from the origin? For what class of Dirichlet problems are such functions natural candidate solutions?
> 2. What characterizes the harmonic functions on $\mathbb{R}^2$ that are independent of $y$ ? Prove your answer. For what class of Dirichlet problems are such functions natural candidate solutions?



##### problem 1

On appropriate subdomains of $\mathbb{C}$, the harmonic functions that are constant on rays from the origin are precisely the functions of the form

$$
u(z)=a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b,
$$

where $a$ and $b$ are real constants.

The reason is that a function that is constant on rays depends only on the angle $\theta=\operatorname{Arg} z$ and not on the radial variable $r=|z|$. The basic harmonic example of this type is $\operatorname{Arg} z$, which is harmonic on a slit plane such as

$$
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0].
$$

By linearity of harmonic functions, every function of the form

$$
a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b
$$

is also harmonic on such a domain. Because $\operatorname{Arg} z$ is constant along each ray from the origin, the same is true of $a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b$.

These functions are natural candidate solutions for Dirichlet problems in regions where the prescribed boundary data is constant on rays, or equivalently depends only on the angular variable and not on the distance from the origin. In such problems one expects the solution to vary with direction but remain unchanged as one moves outward along a fixed ray.

##### problem 2

The harmonic functions on $\mathbb{R}^2$ that are independent of $y$ are precisely the functions of the form

$$
u(x,y)=ax+b,
$$

where $a$ and $b$ are real constants.

To prove this, suppose that $u$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{R}^2$ and is independent of $y$. Then $u$ depends only on $x$, so we may write

$$
u(x,y)=\phi(x)
$$

for some twice-differentiable real-valued function $\phi$.

Because $u$ is independent of $y$, we have

$$
u_y=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_{yy}=0.
$$

Since $u$ is harmonic, it satisfies Laplace's equation:

$$
u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0.
$$

Substitute $u_{yy}=0$:

$$
u_{xx}+0=0,
$$

so

$$
u_{xx}=0.
$$

But $u(x,y)=\phi(x)$, so this means

$$
\phi''(x)=0.
$$

Integrating once gives

$$
\phi'(x)=a,
$$

where $a$ is a real constant. Integrating again gives

$$
\phi(x)=ax+b,
$$

where $b$ is another real constant.

Therefore

$$
u(x,y)=ax+b.
$$

Conversely, any function of the form $ax+b$ is harmonic, since

$$
u_x=a,\qquad u_{xx}=0,\qquad u_y=0,\qquad u_{yy}=0,
$$

and hence

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0.
$$

So these are exactly the harmonic functions on $\mathbb{R}^2$ that are independent of $y$.

Such functions are natural candidate solutions for Dirichlet problems in vertical strips or other regions where the boundary data is constant along horizontal lines and depends only on the $x$-coordinate. In that situation one expects the steady-state temperature to vary only in the horizontal direction, not in the vertical one.



+++++


> [!exercise]
> Let $u=a \operatorname{Arg} z+b$. Show that $u(x, y)=a \operatorname{Arg} z+b$ (for real constants $a, b$ ) is harmonic on $\mathbb{C} \backslash(-\infty, 0]$ and is constant on rays from the origin.




##### solution

Let

$$
u(x,y)=a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b,
$$

where $a$ and $b$ are real constants. We must show two things:

1. $u$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]$.
2. $u$ is constant on rays from the origin.

To prove harmonicity, recall from Example 1(c) that the function

$$
\operatorname{Arg} z
$$

is harmonic on the slit plane

$$
\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0].
$$

Also, constant functions are harmonic, and Proposition 1 says that any linear combination of harmonic functions with real constant coefficients is harmonic.

Now

$$
u(x,y)=a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b
$$

is exactly a linear combination of the harmonic function $\operatorname{Arg} z$ and the constant harmonic function $1$. Therefore $u$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]$.

If we want to write this explicitly in the form used in Proposition 1, we may set

$$
u_1(x,y)=\operatorname{Arg} z
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_2(x,y)=1.
$$

Then

$$
u=a u_1+b u_2,
$$

and since both $u_1$ and $u_2$ are harmonic, so is $u$.

Now we show that $u$ is constant on rays from the origin. A ray from the origin is obtained by fixing the angle $\theta$ and allowing only the radial variable $r$ to vary. Along such a ray, the argument of $z$ does not change:

$$
\operatorname{Arg} z=\theta.
$$

Therefore, on that ray,

$$
\begin{aligned}
u(x,y)
&=a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b\\
&=a\theta+b.
\end{aligned}
$$

The right-hand side depends only on the fixed angle $\theta$ and not on the distance $r$ from the origin. Hence its value is the same at every point of that ray.

So $u(x,y)=a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b$ is constant on rays from the origin.

We conclude that for real constants $a$ and $b$, the function

$$
\boxed{u(x,y)=a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b}
$$

is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]$ and is constant on rays from the origin.



++++




> [!exercise]
> Let $u=a \ln |z|+b$. Show that $u(z)=a \ln |z|+b$ (for real constants $a, b$ ) is harmonic on $\mathbb{C} \backslash\{0\}$ and depends only on $|z|$.



##### solution

Let

$$
u(z)=a\ln|z|+b,
$$

where $a$ and $b$ are real constants.

To show that $u$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}$, recall from Example 1(d) that

$$
\ln|z|
$$

is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}$. Also, constant functions are harmonic, and Proposition 1 says that any linear combination of harmonic functions with real constant coefficients is harmonic.

Now

$$
u(z)=a\ln|z|+b
$$

is a linear combination of the harmonic function $\ln|z|$ and a constant function. Therefore $u$ is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}$.

To show that $u$ depends only on $|z|$, write

$$
r=|z|.
$$

Then

$$
u(z)=a\ln r+b.
$$

This expression involves only the radial variable $r$ and does not involve the argument of $z$. Hence the value of $u$ is determined entirely by the distance of $z$ from the origin.

So if two points $z_1$ and $z_2$ satisfy

$$
|z_1|=|z_2|,
$$

then

$$
\begin{aligned}
u(z_1)
&=a\ln|z_1|+b\\
&=a\ln|z_2|+b\\
&=u(z_2).
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus $u$ is constant on circles centered at the origin, which is exactly what it means to say that $u$ depends only on $|z|$.

Therefore, for real constants $a$ and $b$, the function

$$
\boxed{u(z)=a\ln|z|+b}
$$

is harmonic on $\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}$ and depends only on $|z|$.


++++


We now return to our main topic: that of solving Dirichlet problems. Let us first mention an interesting example of a harmonic function, $u(x, y)= a \operatorname{Arg} z+b$, which is harmonic by Example 1(c) and Proposition 1. Because $\operatorname{Arg} z$ is constant on rays (independent of $r$ ), it follows that $u=a \operatorname{Arg} z+b$ is also constant on rays. (In fact, this is the only harmonic function with such a property. See Exercise 49.) Thus $u$ is a good candidate for a solution of Dirichlet problems in which the boundary data is constant on rays or independent of $r$. We illustrate these ideas with an example.


> [!exercise] Exercise 3: A Dirichlet problem in a quadrant
> 
> Find the steady-state temperature $u(x, y)$ in a large sheet of metal occupying a quadrant of the plane, whose boundary is held at the constant temperatures $100^{\circ}$ on the bottom edge and $50^{\circ}$ on the left edge.
> 
> > [!figure]
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-42_485_542_1401_100.jpg)
> > Dirichlet problem
> > 
> > 
> 
> 




##### solution

From the figure, the metal plate occupies the first quadrant

$$
\Omega=\{(x,y):x>0,\ y>0\},
$$

so points in the region have argument

$$
0<\theta=\operatorname{Arg} z<\frac{\pi}{2}.
$$

The boundary conditions shown in the figure are:

$$
u=100
\qquad\text{on the positive }x\text{-axis},
$$

and

$$
u=50
\qquad\text{on the positive }y\text{-axis}.
$$

Because these boundary values are constant on rays from the origin, it is natural to look for a harmonic solution of the form

$$
u(z)=a\,\operatorname{Arg} z+b.
$$

This function is harmonic on the first quadrant because the first quadrant lies inside the slit plane $\mathbb{C}\setminus(-\infty,0]$, where $\operatorname{Arg} z$ is harmonic.

Now determine the constants $a$ and $b$ from the boundary conditions.

On the positive $x$-axis we have

$$
\operatorname{Arg} z=0.
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
u
&=a\cdot 0+b\\
&=b.
\end{aligned}
$$

But the boundary condition there is $u=100$, so

$$
b=100.
$$

On the positive $y$-axis we have

$$
\operatorname{Arg} z=\frac{\pi}{2}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
u
&=a\frac{\pi}{2}+b\\
&=a\frac{\pi}{2}+100.
\end{aligned}
$$

But the boundary condition there is $u=50$, so

$$
a\frac{\pi}{2}+100=50.
$$

Subtract $100$ from both sides:

$$
a\frac{\pi}{2}=-50.
$$

Now solve for $a$:

$$
a=-\frac{100}{\pi}.
$$

Substitute these constants back into the formula for $u$:

$$
u(z)=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg} z.
$$

Thus the steady-state temperature distribution is

$$
\boxed{u(x,y)=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg} z.}
$$

Since we are in the first quadrant, we may also write

$$
\operatorname{Arg} z=\arctan\!\left(\frac{y}{x}\right),
$$

so equivalently

$$
\boxed{u(x,y)=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\arctan\!\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)}
$$

for $x>0$ and $y>0$.

We can check the boundary values directly:

On $y=0$ with $x>0$, we have $\operatorname{Arg} z=0$, so

$$
u=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\cdot 0=100.
$$

On $x=0$ with $y>0$, we have $\operatorname{Arg} z=\frac{\pi}{2}$, so

$$
u=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\cdot \frac{\pi}{2}=100-50=50.
$$

So this function satisfies both the Laplace equation and the boundary conditions in the figure.




++++



> [!figure] Figure 5: For Exercise 3
> 
> ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-43_452_470_196_132.jpg)
> Figure 5





In contrast to $\operatorname{Arg} z$ we can find harmonic functions which are independent of the argument and depend only on $r=|z|$. An example of such a function is $u(z)=a \ln |z|+b$, where $a$ and $b$ are real constants. By Example $1(\mathrm{~d})$, this function is harmonic in $\mathbb{C} \backslash\{0\}$. It is a good candidate for a solution of Dirichlet problems in which the boundary data is constant on circles. See Exercises 29-32 for illustrations.





> [!exercise] Exercise 4: Dirichlet problem in an infinite strip
> Solve the Dirichlet problem on the infinite strip $\{(x, y): 0<y<1\}$ with boundary conditions $u=0^{\circ}$ on $y=0$ and $u=100^{\circ}$ on $y=1$.
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 6: For Exercise 4
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-43_469_503_1047_101.jpg)
> > Figure 6 Dirichlet problem in Example 4.
> 
> 
> 



##### solution

We seek a harmonic function on the strip

$$
\{(x,y):0<y<1\}
$$

such that

$$
u(x,0)=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u(x,1)=100
$$

for all $x$.

Because the boundary values are constant along each horizontal boundary line and do not depend on $x$, it is natural to look for a solution that is independent of $x$. So assume

$$
u(x,y)=\phi(y)
$$

for some function $\phi$ of one variable.

Since $u$ is independent of $x$, we have

$$
u_x=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_{xx}=0.
$$

For $u$ to be harmonic, it must satisfy Laplace's equation:

$$
u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0.
$$

Substituting $u_{xx}=0$, we obtain

$$
0+u_{yy}=0,
$$

so

$$
u_{yy}=0.
$$

But $u(x,y)=\phi(y)$, so this means

$$
\phi''(y)=0.
$$

Integrating once gives

$$
\phi'(y)=A,
$$

where $A$ is a constant. Integrating again gives

$$
\phi(y)=Ay+B,
$$

where $B$ is another constant.

Therefore the candidate solution has the form

$$
u(x,y)=Ay+B.
$$

Now impose the boundary conditions. On $y=0$ we require

$$
u(x,0)=A\cdot 0+B=B=0,
$$

so

$$
B=0.
$$

On $y=1$ we require

$$
u(x,1)=A\cdot 1+0=A=100,
$$

so

$$
A=100.
$$

Thus

$$
u(x,y)=100y.
$$

We now verify this directly:

$$
u_x=0,
\qquad
u_{xx}=0,
\qquad
u_y=100,
\qquad
u_{yy}=0.
$$

Hence

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0+0=0,
$$

so $u$ is harmonic on the strip. Also,

$$
u(x,0)=100\cdot 0=0
$$

and

$$
u(x,1)=100\cdot 1=100.
$$

Therefore the steady-state temperature distribution is

$$
\boxed{u(x,y)=100y.}
$$


++++




In Example 4 we used a harmonic function that was independent of $x$. Similarly, we can find harmonic functions that are independent of $y$ (hence $u_{y}=0$ and $u_{y y}=0$ ). You can show in this case that $u=a x+b$, where $a$ and $b$ are real constants. This function is a good candidate for solving Dirichlet problems in infinite vertical strips with constant boundary data. See problems 25 and 26 for illustrations.





# 2.5.5 Harmonic Conjugates, Isotherms, and Heat Flow


> [!review]
> 1.) What are isotherms in a steady-state heat distribution on a plate, and in terms of the temperature function $u(x, y)$, what curves do they correspond to?
> 2.) Why are the curves of heat flow in a steady-state temperature distribution orthogonal to the isotherms?
> 3.) How can a harmonic conjugate $v$ of a steady-state temperature distribution $u$ be used to determine the curves of heat flow on the plate, and why does this method work?
> 



##### problem 1

In a steady-state heat distribution on a plate, an isotherm is a curve along which the temperature is constant. If the temperature function is

$$
u(x,y),
$$

then the isotherms are exactly the level curves of $u$. In other words, for a fixed temperature value $T$, the corresponding isotherm is the curve determined by

$$
u(x,y)=T.
$$

So isotherms are the loci of points in the plate having the same temperature.

##### problem 2

The curves of heat flow are orthogonal to the isotherms because heat flows in the direction of greatest decrease of temperature, and that direction is perpendicular to level curves of the temperature function.

More precisely, from vector calculus we know that the gradient

$$
\nabla u=(u_x,u_y)
$$

is perpendicular to the level curves of $u(x,y)$. Since an isotherm is a level curve of $u$, the gradient is normal to each isotherm.

Fourier's law says that heat flows in the direction of

$$
-\nabla u,
$$

that is, from hotter regions toward colder ones. Because $-\nabla u$ is just the negative of $\nabla u$, it has the same line of action, so it is also perpendicular to the isotherms.

Therefore the curves of heat flow, whose tangent directions follow $-\nabla u$, are orthogonal to the isotherms.

##### problem 3

If $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of the steady-state temperature distribution $u$, then the curves of heat flow can be found by taking the level curves of $v$:

$$
v(x,y)=C.
$$

This works because if $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, then

$$
f=u+i v
$$

is analytic, and Theorem 2 tells us that the level curves of $u$ and the level curves of $v$ are orthogonal at every point where

$$
f'(z)\neq 0.
$$

But the level curves of $u$ are precisely the isotherms. Since curves of heat flow are orthogonal to the isotherms, and the level curves of $v$ are also orthogonal to the isotherms, the level curves of $v$ coincide with the curves of heat flow.

So the practical method is:

find a harmonic conjugate $v$ of the temperature function $u$, then solve

$$
v(x,y)=C
$$

to obtain the flow lines.

The reason this method works is the combination of two facts: heat flow follows a direction orthogonal to the level curves of $u$, and the harmonic conjugate $v$ has level curves orthogonal to those same curves.



+++++



In Example 3, the temperature of the boundary is kept at two constant values, $100^{\circ}$ and $50^{\circ}$. Our physical intuition tells us that, because the plate is insulated, the temperature of the points inside the plate will vary between these two values and will equal those values only at the boundary. It is natural to ask for those points inside the plate with the same temperature $u(x, y)=T$, where $50<T<100$. These points lie on curves inside the plate, called curves of constant temperature or isotherms. Isotherms have many practical applications. Computing them will lead us to interesting properties of harmonic functions.




You should recall from vector calculus that the gradient vector $\nabla u=\left(u_{x}, u_{y}\right)$ points in the direction of greatest change in a function. The gradient is perpendicular to level curves of $u(x, y)$. Fourier's law states that heat flows along $-\nabla u$, and thus curves of heat flow are orthogonal to level curves of $u$, and hence coincident with level curves of $v$.




> [!exercise] Exercise 5: Isotherms
> 
> Find the isotherms in Exercise 3.
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 7: For Exercise 5
> > 
> > ![](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-44_487_543_244_106.jpg)
> > 
> > Figure 7 Isotherms in Example 5.
> 
> 
> 




##### solution

In Exercise 3, the steady-state temperature in the first quadrant was found to be

$$
u(x,y)=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg} z.
$$

The isotherms are the curves along which the temperature is constant. So let

$$
u(x,y)=T,
$$

where $50<T<100$.

Substitute the formula for $u$:

$$
100-\frac{100}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg} z=T.
$$

Now solve for $\operatorname{Arg} z$.

Subtract $100$ from both sides:

$$
-\frac{100}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg} z=T-100.
$$

Multiply both sides by $-\dfrac{\pi}{100}$:

$$
\operatorname{Arg} z=\frac{\pi}{100}(100-T).
$$

Thus each isotherm is given by a constant value of $\operatorname{Arg} z$. That means the isotherms are rays from the origin.

If we write

$$
\theta=\frac{\pi}{100}(100-T),
$$

then the isotherm corresponding to temperature $T$ is

$$
\operatorname{Arg} z=\theta.
$$

Since we are in the first quadrant, this is equivalently

$$
\arctan\!\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)=\theta,
$$

so

$$
\frac{y}{x}=\tan\theta,
$$

and therefore

$$
y=x\tan\theta.
$$

Substituting the expression for $\theta$, we may write the isotherms as

$$
\boxed{y=x\tan\!\left(\frac{\pi}{100}(100-T)\right)},
\qquad 50<T<100.
$$

So the isotherms in Exercise 3 are straight rays issuing from the origin. As $T$ decreases from $100$ to $50$, the angle increases from $0$ to $\dfrac{\pi}{2}$, so these rays sweep through the first quadrant from the positive $x$-axis toward the positive $y$-axis.




++++



Related to the topic of isotherms is the topic of curves of heat flow. These are the curves along which the heat is flowing inside the plate. To determine these curves, we use Fourier's law of heat conduction, which states that heat flows from hot to cold in the direction in which the temperature difference is the greatest. If along the isotherms the temperature difference is zero, then it should make sense that in the direction perpendicular to the isotherms the temperature difference is the greatest. Hence the curves of heat flow are orthogonal to the isotherms.

So to find the curves of heat flow in a plate, it is enough to find a harmonic conjugate $v(x, y)$ of the steady-state temperature distribution $u(x, y)$, since by Theorem 2 the level curves of $v$ are orthogonal to the level curves of $u$ We illustrate this process with an example.


> [!exercise] Exercise 6: Curves of Heat Flow
> 
> 
> Find the curves of heat flow in Example 3.
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 8: For Exercise 6
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-45_477_510_300_76.jpg)
> > Figure 8 Curves of heat flow in Example 6, along with the isotherms.
> 
> 
> 




##### solution

In Exercise 3, the steady-state temperature distribution in the first quadrant was found to be

$$
u(x,y)=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg} z.
$$

To find the curves of heat flow, we look for a harmonic conjugate of $u$. Since

$$
\log z=\ln|z|+i\operatorname{Arg} z,
$$

the function $\operatorname{Arg} z$ is a harmonic conjugate of $\ln|z|$. Therefore, by the result that if $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, then $-u$ is a harmonic conjugate of $v$, a harmonic conjugate of $\operatorname{Arg} z$ is

$$
-\ln|z|.
$$

Now multiply by the constant coefficient appearing in $u$. Since

$$
u(x,y)=100-\frac{100}{\pi}\operatorname{Arg} z,
$$

a harmonic conjugate of $u$ is

$$
v(x,y)=-\frac{100}{\pi}\bigl(-\ln|z|\bigr)=\frac{100}{\pi}\ln|z|,
$$

up to an additive real constant.

Thus we may take

$$
v(x,y)=\frac{100}{\pi}\ln|z|.
$$

The curves of heat flow are the level curves of $v$, so set

$$
v(x,y)=C.
$$

Then

$$
\frac{100}{\pi}\ln|z|=C.
$$

Multiply both sides by $\dfrac{\pi}{100}$:

$$
\ln|z|=\frac{\pi C}{100}.
$$

Exponentiating gives

$$
|z|=e^{\pi C/100}.
$$

Since $e^{\pi C/100}$ is a positive constant, we may write it simply as

$$
|z|=R,
\qquad R>0.
$$

Therefore the curves of heat flow are circles centered at the origin. Because the plate occupies only the first quadrant, the relevant portions are the quarter-circles

$$
x^2+y^2=R^2,
\qquad x>0,\ y>0.
$$

So the answer is that the curves of heat flow in Example 3 are the quarter-circles centered at the origin in the first quadrant. They are orthogonal to the isotherms, which were the rays from the origin.



++++




# 2.5.6 Conformal Mappings



> [!review]
> 1.) What strategic idea underlies the method of conformal mappings for solving Dirichlet problems?
> 2.) What does it mean for a mapping $w=f(z)$ to be a conformal mapping of a region $\Omega$, and what geometric properties does such a mapping have that make it suitable for transforming a Dirichlet problem on $\Omega$ into one posed on the image region?
> 3.) If $U$ is a harmonic function on a region $\Omega^{\prime}$ and we pull it back to a region $\Omega$ via a mapping $w=f(z)$ (forming $u(z)=U(f(z))$ ), under what hypotheses on $f$ is $u$ guaranteed to be harmonic on $\Omega$ ? Prove your answer.





+++++



We conclude our panoramic overview of Dirichlet problems and their solutions by mentioning the method of conformal mappings, which is of wide use in complex analysis. In the past you have certainly used methods to transform a difficult problem to one with a known solution or whose solution is easier to find. For example, some integrals are simplified by using changes of variables, some differential equations are simplified by applying a Laplace transform, and so on. In solving Dirichlet problems, it is sometimes advantageous to map the region under consideration to a simpler region or one on which the transformed problem is easier to solve. This is the idea behind the method of conformal mappings, which we now explain.

Let a Dirichlet problem be given on a region $\Omega$ with boundary $\Gamma$. Suppose that we want to solve this problem by somehow transforming it first to the $w$ plane by means of a mapping $w=f(z)$, where $f$ is analytic in $\Omega$. If $f^{\prime}(z) \neq 0$ for all $z$ in $\Omega$, we call $f$ a conformal mapping of $\Omega$. Conformal mappings have important properties that will be studied in detail in Chapter 6. For example, if $f$ is conformal, then the image of $\Omega, \Omega^{\prime}=f[\Omega]$, is also a region (open, connected set); moreover, if $f$ is one-to-one, then $f$ will map $\Gamma$ onto $\Gamma^{\prime}$, the boundary of $\Omega^{\prime}$. In the examples of this section, these properties can be checked on a case-by-case basis.

In transforming the Dirichlet problem, we need to know what happens to Laplace's equation under our transformation $w=f(z)$ and what happens to the boundary conditions. Because $f$ maps boundary to boundary, the boundary conditions on $\Gamma$ will be transformed into boundary conditions on $\Gamma^{\prime}$ as we will explain shortly. However, the most important feature of this method is stated in the next theorem and tells us that Laplace's equation is invariant under a change of variables using a conformal mapping.



> [!NOTE]
> To understand the meaning of $U \circ f(z)$ where $f$ is complexvalued and $U$ is a function of two variables, write $U \circ f(z)=U(\operatorname{Re} f(z), \operatorname{Im} f(z))$. For example, if $f(z)= e^{z}=e^{x} \cos y+i e^{x} \sin y$ and $U(s, t)=s t$, then $U \circ f(z)= e^{2 x} \cos y \sin y$.





> [!Theorem] Theorem 3: Invariance of Laplace's Equation
> Suppose that $f$ is an analytic mapping of $\Omega$ into $\Omega^{\prime}$ and $U$ is a harmonic function on $\Omega^{\prime}$. Then $U \circ f$ is harmonic in $\Omega$. Thus, if $U$ satisfies $\Delta U=0$ on $\Omega^{\prime}$, then $u=U \circ f$ satisfies $\Delta u=0$ on $\Omega$.

**Proof** 




> [!review]
> Given a Dirichlet problem on a region $\Omega$ (Laplace's equation with prescribed boundary data $b$ on $\Gamma$ ), and an analytic mapping $w=f(z)$ that is one-to-one on $\Omega$ and its boundary, how can $f$ be used to transform the problem into a Dirichlet problem posed on the image region, and how does a solution of the transformed problem then yield a solution of the original? Prove your answer.



##### solution

Suppose we are given the Dirichlet problem

$$
\Delta u=0 \quad \text{in } \Omega,
\qquad
u(z)=b(z) \quad \text{for } z\in\Gamma,
$$

where $\Gamma$ is the boundary of $\Omega$. Assume that $w=f(z)$ is analytic on $\Omega$ and one-to-one on both $\Omega$ and its boundary $\Gamma$.

The strategy is to transfer the problem from the $z$-plane to the $w$-plane, solve the transformed problem there, and then pull the solution back to $\Omega$.

First, map the region and its boundary:

$$
\Omega' = f[\Omega],
\qquad
\Gamma' = f[\Gamma].
$$

Because $f$ is one-to-one, it has an inverse function

$$
f^{-1}
$$

defined on $\Omega'$ and on its boundary $\Gamma'$.

Now we transport the boundary data. If $w\in\Gamma'$, then $f^{-1}(w)\in\Gamma$, so the original boundary function $b$ induces a new boundary function on $\Gamma'$ given by

$$
B(w)=b\bigl(f^{-1}(w)\bigr).
$$

Thus the transformed Dirichlet problem on the image region is

$$
\Delta U=0 \quad \text{in } \Omega',
\qquad
U(w)=b\bigl(f^{-1}(w)\bigr) \quad \text{for } w\in\Gamma'.
$$

This is the problem one solves in the $w$-plane.

Once a solution $U$ of the transformed problem is found, we define a function on the original region by composing with $f$:

$$
u(z)=U(f(z))=U\circ f(z).
$$

We now show that this $u$ solves the original Dirichlet problem.

First, $U$ is harmonic on $\Omega'$ and $f$ is analytic on $\Omega$, so by the invariance theorem for Laplace's equation,

$$
u=U\circ f
$$

is harmonic on $\Omega$. Therefore

$$
\Delta u=0 \quad \text{in } \Omega.
$$

Next we check the boundary condition. Let $z\in\Gamma$. Then $f(z)\in\Gamma'$, so by the transformed boundary condition,

$$
U(f(z))=b\bigl(f^{-1}(f(z))\bigr).
$$

Since $f$ is one-to-one, we have

$$
f^{-1}(f(z))=z.
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
u(z)
&=U(f(z))\\
&=b\bigl(f^{-1}(f(z))\bigr)\\
&=b(z).
\end{aligned}
$$

So $u$ satisfies the prescribed boundary values on $\Gamma$.

Hence the method is:

1. map $\Omega$ and $\Gamma$ to $\Omega'$ and $\Gamma'$ using $w=f(z)$;
2. transfer the boundary data to $\Gamma'$ as $b\circ f^{-1}$;
3. solve the transformed Dirichlet problem for $U$ on $\Omega'$;
4. pull the solution back by setting $u=U\circ f$.

The proof that this works is exactly what we showed above: $u=U\circ f$ is harmonic in $\Omega$ by invariance of Laplace's equation, and on the boundary it satisfies

$$
u(z)=b(z).
$$

Therefore $u$ is a solution of the original Dirichlet problem on $\Omega$.



+++++



Now suppose that you want to use a conformal mapping $w=f(z)$ to solve the Dirichlet problem $\Delta u=0$ in $\Omega$ and $u(z)=b(z)$ on the boundary $\Gamma$ of $\Omega$. Suppose also that $f$ is one-to-one on $\Omega$ and its boundary $\Gamma$. Here is how the method works (see _Figure 9_ as you read through the steps).


> [!figure] Figure 9: For Proof to Theorem 3
> 
> ![](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-46_519_1162_1082_766.jpg)
> 
> The conformal mapping method. If $f(z)$ is analytic and one-to-one on $\Omega$ and its boundary $\Gamma$, then $\Omega^{\prime}= f[\Omega]$ is a region with boundary $\Gamma^{\prime}=f[\Gamma]$. The boundary function $b(z)$ ( $z$ on $\Gamma$ ) is used to define a boundary function $b \circ f^{-1}(w)$ for all $w$ on $\Gamma$, where $f^{-1}$ is the inverse of $f$.



Step 1: Describe clearly the region $\Omega^{\prime}=f[\Omega]$ and its boundary $\Gamma^{\prime}=f[\Gamma]$ in the $w$-plane.
Step 2: Since $f$ is one-to-one, we have an inverse function $f^{-1}$ defined on $\Omega^{\prime}$ and $\Gamma^{\prime}$. For $w$ on $\Gamma^{\prime}, f^{-1}(w)$ is on $\Gamma$ and so we can define the function $b \circ f^{-1}(w)$ for all $w$ on $\Gamma^{\prime}$. This determines the boundary values on $\Gamma^{\prime}$.
Step 3: Set up and solve the Dirichlet problem on $\Omega^{\prime}$ consisting of Laplace's equation $\Delta U(w)=0$ for all $w$ in $\Omega^{\prime}$ and $U(w)=b \circ f^{-1}(w)$ for all $w$ on $\Gamma^{\prime}$. (This is our transformed Dirichlet problem.)
Step 4 Let $u(z)=U \circ f(z)$ for all $z$ in $\Omega$. Then $u(z)$ is a solution of out original Dirichlet problem on $\Omega$. Indeed, by Theorem 3, $u$ is harmonic in $\Omega$. For $z$ on the boundary $\Gamma$, we have $f(z)$ on the boundary $\Gamma^{\prime}$, and using the fact that $U(w)=b \circ f^{-1}(w)$ on $\Gamma^{\prime}$, we obtain for $z$ on $\Gamma, u(z)=U \circ f(z)= b \circ f^{-1}(f(z))=b(z)$. Hence $u$ satisfies the desired boundary condition.

In most examples, Steps 2 and 3 can be done without actually computives $f^{-1}$, as we illustrate in our next example.




> [!exercise] Exercise 7
> (a) Solve the Dirichlet problem in the semi-infinite strip $\Omega$ shown in _Figure 10_.
> (b) Discuss the isotherms and lines of heat flow of the solution.
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 10
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-47_468_498_188_96.jpg)
> > Dirichlet problem in Exercise 7.
> 
> 


##### solution

Let

$$
\Omega=\left\{x+i y:-\frac{\pi}{2}<x<\frac{\pi}{2},\ y>0\right\}.
$$

The boundary conditions in _Figure 10_ are

$$
u=100
\quad \text{on } x=-\frac{\pi}{2} \text{ and on } -\frac{\pi}{2}<x<0,\ y=0,
$$

and

$$
u=0
\quad \text{on } x=\frac{\pi}{2} \text{ and on } 0<x<\frac{\pi}{2},\ y=0.
$$

The value at the single point $z=0$ is irrelevant.

For part (a), use the conformal map

$$
w=f(z)=\sin z.
$$

If $z=x+i y$, then

$$
w=\sin z=\sin x \cosh y+i \cos x \sinh y.
$$

Since $-\dfrac{\pi}{2}<x<\dfrac{\pi}{2}$, we have $\cos x>0$, and therefore

$$
\operatorname{Im} w=\cos x \sinh y>0
$$

for every $z \in \Omega$. Hence $f[\Omega]$ is the upper half-plane.

Now check the boundary correspondence, as in _Figure 11_. On the left side $x=-\dfrac{\pi}{2}$,

$$
w=\sin \left(-\frac{\pi}{2}+i y\right)=-\cosh y \leq-1 .
$$

On the lower-left part of the bottom edge, where $-\dfrac{\pi}{2} \leq x \leq 0$ and $y=0$,

$$
w=\sin x \in[-1,0].
$$

So the part of the boundary where the temperature is $100^{\circ}$ maps to the negative real axis. Likewise, on the right side $x=\dfrac{\pi}{2}$,

$$
w=\sin \left(\frac{\pi}{2}+i y\right)=\cosh y \geq 1,
$$

and on the lower-right part, where $0 \leq x \leq \dfrac{\pi}{2}$ and $y=0$,

$$
w=\sin x \in[0,1].
$$

So the part of the boundary where the temperature is $0^{\circ}$ maps to the positive real axis.

The transformed Dirichlet problem in the $w$-plane is therefore

$$
\Delta U=0 \quad \text { in } \operatorname{Im} w>0,
$$

with boundary values

$$
U=100 \quad \text { on }(-\infty, 0),
\qquad
U=0 \quad \text { on }(0, \infty) .
$$

Using the principal argument on the upper half-plane, $0<\operatorname{Arg} w<\pi$, we obtain

$$
U(w)=\frac{100}{\pi} \operatorname{Arg} w .
$$

Indeed, $\operatorname{Arg} w=\pi$ on the negative real axis and $\operatorname{Arg} w=0$ on the positive real axis.

Therefore

$$
u(z)=U(\sin z)=\frac{100}{\pi} \operatorname{Arg}(\sin z)
$$

solves the original problem. If we write $w=s+i t=\sin z$, then

$$
s=\sin x \cosh y,
\qquad
t=\cos x \sinh y,
$$

so, using _Figure 12_,

$$
u(x, y)=\frac{100}{\pi} \cot ^{-1}\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)
=\frac{100}{\pi} \cot ^{-1}\left(\frac{\sin x \cosh y}{\cos x \sinh y}\right)
=\frac{100}{\pi} \cot ^{-1}(\tan x \operatorname{coth} y) .
$$

Thus

$$
\boxed{u(x, y)=\frac{100}{\pi} \operatorname{Arg}(\sin (x+i y))
=\frac{100}{\pi} \cot ^{-1}(\tan x \operatorname{coth} y)}
$$

for

$$
-\frac{\pi}{2}<x<\frac{\pi}{2},
\qquad
y>0.
$$

For part (b), the isotherms are the level curves $u(x, y)=T$, where $0<T<100$. Since

$$
\frac{100}{\pi} \operatorname{Arg}(\sin z)=T,
$$

we get

$$
\operatorname{Arg}(\sin z)=\frac{\pi T}{100} .
$$

In the $w$-plane these are the rays

$$
\operatorname{Arg} w=\frac{\pi T}{100} .
$$

Pulling them back by $w=\sin z$ gives

$$
\tan \left(\frac{\pi T}{100}\right)
=\frac{\operatorname{Im}(\sin z)}{\operatorname{Re}(\sin z)}
=\frac{\cos x \sinh y}{\sin x \cosh y}
=\cot x \tanh y .
$$

Hence the isotherms satisfy

$$
\boxed{\tan x=\cot \left(\frac{\pi T}{100}\right) \tanh y},
\qquad 0<T<100 .
$$

Equivalently,

$$
x=\tan ^{-1}\left(\cot \left(\frac{\pi T}{100}\right) \tanh y\right) .
$$

These are the curves shown in _Figure 13_. Each one starts at the origin, and as $y \to \infty$ it approaches the vertical line

$$
x=\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{\pi T}{100} .
$$

To get the lines of heat flow, take a harmonic conjugate of $U$. Since

$$
-i \operatorname{Log} w=\operatorname{Arg} w-i \ln |w|,
$$

a harmonic conjugate of $\operatorname{Arg} w$ is $-\ln |w|$. Thus we may take

$$
V(w)=-\frac{100}{\pi} \ln |w| .
$$

The curves of heat flow are the level curves of $V$, so in the $w$-plane they are the semicircles

$$
|w|=R,
\qquad
R>0,
\qquad
\operatorname{Im} w \geq 0 .
$$

Pulling these back under $w=\sin z$ gives

$$
|\sin z|=R .
$$

Since

$$
|\sin (x+i y)|^{2}
=\sin ^{2} x \cosh ^{2} y+\cos ^{2} x \sinh ^{2} y
=\sin ^{2} x+\sinh ^{2} y,
$$

the lines of heat flow are

$$
\boxed{\sin ^{2} x+\sinh ^{2} y=R^{2}},
\qquad R>0 .
$$

For $0<R \leq 1$ these are arches meeting the bottom edge, and for $R>1$ they are larger arches joining the two vertical sides. They are orthogonal to the isotherms because they arise as level curves of a harmonic conjugate.


The following static plot shows a representative family of isotherms and curves of heat flow in the strip.

```python title:"exercise7_isotherms_heatflow_plot.py"
from pathlib import Path

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np


def isotherm_x(y, temperature):
    theta = np.pi * temperature / 100.0
    return np.arctan((1.0 / np.tan(theta)) * np.tanh(y))


def heat_flow_y(x, radius):
    return np.arcsinh(np.sqrt(np.maximum(0.0, radius**2 - np.sin(x) ** 2)))


def main():
    chapter_dir = Path(__file__).resolve().parent
    output_path = chapter_dir / "images" / "exercise7_isotherms_heatflow_python_static.png"

    x_min = -np.pi / 2
    x_max = np.pi / 2
    y_max = 3.5

    fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(7.2, 8.6), dpi=220)

    ax.fill(
        [x_min, x_min, x_max, x_max],
        [0.0, y_max, y_max, 0.0],
        color="#d8e8f7",
        alpha=0.6,
        zorder=0,
    )
    ax.plot([x_min, x_min], [0.0, y_max], color="#1b4fd1", linewidth=2.2, zorder=2)
    ax.plot([x_max, x_max], [0.0, y_max], color="#1b4fd1", linewidth=2.2, zorder=2)
    ax.plot([x_min, x_max], [0.0, 0.0], color="#1b4fd1", linewidth=2.2, zorder=2)

    ax.axhline(0.0, color="black", linewidth=1.2, zorder=1)
    ax.axvline(0.0, color="black", linewidth=1.0, alpha=0.7, zorder=1)

    y_vals = np.linspace(0.0, y_max, 700)
    temperatures = [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80, 90]
    for temperature in temperatures:
        x_vals = isotherm_x(y_vals, temperature)
        ax.plot(
            x_vals,
            y_vals,
            color="#444444",
            linewidth=1.45,
            linestyle=(0, (6, 5)),
            zorder=3,
        )

        x_limit = np.pi / 2 - np.pi * temperature / 100.0
        ax.text(
            x_limit,
            y_max + 0.08,
            f"{temperature}\N{DEGREE SIGN}",
            ha="center",
            va="bottom",
            fontsize=10.5,
            color="#333333",
        )

    radii = [0.18, 0.32, 0.52, 0.9, 1.45, 2.1, 3.0]
    for radius in radii:
        if radius <= 1.0:
            x_vals = np.linspace(-np.arcsin(radius), np.arcsin(radius), 700)
        else:
            x_vals = np.linspace(x_min, x_max, 900)
        y_vals_curve = heat_flow_y(x_vals, radius)
        ax.plot(x_vals, y_vals_curve, color="#111111", linewidth=1.85, zorder=4)

    ax.text(x_min - 0.05, 3.0, "u = 100°", ha="right", va="center", fontsize=13)
    ax.text(x_max + 0.05, 3.0, "u = 0°", ha="left", va="center", fontsize=13)
    ax.text(-0.82, -0.18, "u = 100°", ha="center", va="top", fontsize=13)
    ax.text(0.82, -0.18, "u = 0°", ha="center", va="top", fontsize=13)

    ax.set_title("Exercise 7: Isotherms and Curves of Heat Flow", fontsize=15, pad=12)
    ax.set_xlim(x_min - 0.2, x_max + 0.2)
    ax.set_ylim(-0.35, y_max + 0.35)
    ax.set_xticks([-np.pi / 2, 0.0, np.pi / 2])
    ax.set_xticklabels([r"$-\pi/2$", r"$0$", r"$\pi/2$"], fontsize=12)
    ax.set_yticks([1.0, 2.0, 3.0])
    ax.set_yticklabels([r"$1$", r"$2$", r"$3$"], fontsize=12)
    ax.set_xlabel(r"$x$", fontsize=14)
    ax.set_ylabel(r"$y$", fontsize=14, rotation=0, labelpad=12)
    ax.grid(True, color="#aeb6bf", linewidth=0.8, alpha=0.6)

    for spine in ("top", "right"):
        ax.spines[spine].set_visible(False)

    fig.subplots_adjust(top=0.93, left=0.14, right=0.90, bottom=0.08)
    fig.savefig(output_path)
    plt.close(fig)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
