++++




> [!review]
> Why might the boundary data of a Dirichlet problem fail to determine a unique solution, and what additional condition on the solution restores uniqueness?




##### solution

Boundary data alone can fail to determine a unique solution because one may be able to add a nonzero harmonic function that vanishes on the boundary and still preserve both Laplace's equation and the prescribed boundary values.

More precisely, if $u$ solves a Dirichlet problem and $\phi$ is harmonic in the region with

$$
\phi=0
\quad \text{on the boundary},
$$

then

$$
\psi=u+\phi
$$

is also harmonic and has the same boundary data as $u$. Thus the same boundary values may correspond to more than one harmonic function.

This is exactly what happens in Example 3: the function $x y$ is harmonic and vanishes on the boundary of the first quadrant, so if $u$ is one solution, then

$$
u(x,y)+x y
$$

is another solution with the same boundary values.

The additional condition that restores uniqueness is to require the solution to be bounded. This boundedness condition acts like a boundary condition at infinity and rules out extra functions such as $x y$, which become unbounded. With the boundary values together with boundedness, the Dirichlet problem has a unique solution.



+++++


> [!exercise]
> Show that if $u(x, y)$ is the solution to the Dirichlet problem of Example 3 (Laplace's equation on the first quadrant with $u=100$ on the positive $x$-axis and $u=50$ on the positive $y$-axis), then $\psi(x, y)=u(x, y)+x y$ is also a solution.



##### solution

We are told that $u(x, y)$ solves the Dirichlet problem of Example 3 on the first quadrant. Thus

$$
\Delta u=0
$$

in the first quadrant, with boundary values

$$
u(x,0)=100
\quad \text{for } x>0,
\qquad
u(0,y)=50
\quad \text{for } y>0.
$$

Define

$$
\psi(x,y)=u(x,y)+x y.
$$

We show that $\psi$ satisfies the same Dirichlet problem.

First, $\psi$ satisfies Laplace's equation. Since $u$ is harmonic,

$$
\Delta u=0.
$$

Also,

$$
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}(x y)=0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}(x y)=0,
$$

so

$$
\Delta(x y)=0.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta \psi
=\Delta(u+x y)
=\Delta u+\Delta(x y)
=0+0
=0.
$$

Thus $\psi$ is harmonic in the first quadrant.

Now check the boundary values. On the positive $x$-axis, where $y=0$,

$$
\psi(x,0)=u(x,0)+x \cdot 0=u(x,0)=100.
$$

On the positive $y$-axis, where $x=0$,

$$
\psi(0,y)=u(0,y)+0 \cdot y=u(0,y)=50.
$$

So $\psi$ has exactly the same boundary values as $u$.

Hence $\psi(x,y)=u(x,y)+x y$ is also a solution of the Dirichlet problem in Example 3.



++++





So far we have used our knowledge of analytic functions to solve Dirichlet problems by guessing the solution. Guessing is certainly a legitimate method that you have used in solving differential equations and computing indefinite integrals. Further development of the theory of analytic and harmonic functions will be necessary to tackle more general Dirichlet problems. Topics such as the Poisson integral formula, Fourier series, and conformal mappings are examples of theories and tools that will provide us with systematic ways for solving Dirichlet problems.

Other issues that we need to address concern the uniqueness of the solution of a Dirichlet problem. Consider the problem in Example 3, whose solution is $u(x, y)=-\frac{100}{\pi} \tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)+100$. If we add to this solution the harmonic function $\phi(x, y)=x y$, we obtain the harmonic function $\psi(x, y)= u(x, y)+x y$, which solves Laplace's equation. Moreover, because $x y$ vanishes on the boundary of the first quadrant, $\psi(x, y)$ and $u(x, y)$ have the same boundary values. Thus, $\psi$ is another solution of the Dirichlet problem in Example 3. This is quite disturbing since we want to think of the solution ${ }^{25}$
representing a temperature distribution and as such it must be unique. As it turns out, if in addition to the boundary conditions in a Dirichlet problem we add the condition that the solution be bounded (which is effectively a boundary condition at infinity), then the solution will be unique. For example, with this additional boundedness assumption, we can no longer add the function $x y$ to the solution in Example 3 to obtain another solution.

Uniqueness results for the solution of Dirichlet problems will be derived from theoretical properties of analytic and harmonic functions, including results such as the maximum principle. All these topics will be addressed as part of the remainder of this book.





# Exercises 2.5

> [!exercise] Exercise 8
> In problemss 1-12, determine the set on which the given function is harmonic. You may verify Laplace's equation (1) directly or use Theorem 1.
> 
> 1. $x^{2}-y^{2}+2 x-y$.
> 2. $\frac{1}{x^{2}-y^{2}}$.
> 3. $e^{x} \cos y$.
> 4. $\frac{y}{x^{2}+y^{2}}$.
> 5. $\frac{1}{x+y}$.
> 6. $\sinh x \cos y$.
> 7. $\cos x \cosh y$.
> 8. $e^{2 x} \cos (2 y)$.
> 9. $e^{x^{2}-y^{2}} \cos (2 x y)$.
> 10. $\ln \left(x^{2}+y^{2}\right)$.
> 11. $\ln \left((x-1)^{2}+y^{2}\right)$.
> 12. $\arg _{\frac{\pi}{2}} z$.



##### solution

In these problems there are two standard ways to determine whether a function is harmonic.

The first method is to verify Laplace's equation directly. If a function is given explicitly in terms of $x$ and $y$ in a simple algebraic form, this is often the most efficient approach: compute

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}
$$

and check whether it is equal to $0$ on the domain of the function. This method is especially useful when the expression is a polynomial, a simple rational function, or a function that does not obviously come from a familiar analytic function. It is also the natural method when one suspects that the function may fail to be harmonic, because it can show directly that $\Delta u\ne 0$.

The second method is to use Theorem 1, which says that the real and imaginary parts of an analytic function are harmonic. This method is preferable when the given function is immediately recognizable as the real part or imaginary part of a standard analytic function such as

$$
e^z,\qquad \sin z,\qquad \cos z,\qquad \sinh z,\qquad \cosh z,\qquad \frac{1}{z},\qquad e^{z^2},
$$

or a branch of $\log z$. In that situation, it is usually much faster to identify the analytic parent function and then invoke Theorem 1 than to differentiate twice and simplify.

So the general principle is this: if the function has a clear analytic source, use Theorem 1; if it does not, or if you need to show it is not harmonic, compute the Laplacian directly.

##### problem 1

Consider

$$
u(x,y)=x^2-y^2+2x-y.
$$

Here the fastest method is to verify Laplace's equation directly, because the function is just a polynomial in $x$ and $y$, so the derivatives are immediate.

Compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2-y^2+2x-y)\\
&=2x+2,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2-y^2+2x-y)\\
&=-2y-1.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now compute the second partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(2x+2)\\
&=2,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-2y-1)\\
&=-2.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta u
&=u_{xx}+u_{yy}\\
&=2+(-2)\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $u$ has continuous first and second partial derivatives everywhere, $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

##### problem 2

Now consider

$$
u(x,y)=\frac{1}{x^2-y^2}.
$$

Here it is better to verify Laplace's equation directly, because the function is not the obvious real or imaginary part of a standard analytic function, and we also need to decide whether it is harmonic anywhere.

First determine where the function is defined:

$$
x^2-y^2\ne 0.
$$

That means

$$
x\ne y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
x\ne -y.
$$

So the natural domain is

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{x+iy:x=y\text{ or }x=-y\}.
$$

Now compute derivatives. Write

$$
u(x,y)=(x^2-y^2)^{-1}.
$$

Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2-y^2)^{-1}\\
&=-1\cdot (x^2-y^2)^{-2}\cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2-y^2)\\
&=-(x^2-y^2)^{-2}(2x)\\
&=-\frac{2x}{(x^2-y^2)^2},
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2-y^2)^{-1}\\
&=-1\cdot (x^2-y^2)^{-2}\cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2-y^2)\\
&=-(x^2-y^2)^{-2}(-2y)\\
&=\frac{2y}{(x^2-y^2)^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate again:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[-2x(x^2-y^2)^{-2}\right]\\
&=-2(x^2-y^2)^{-2}+(-2x)\left[-2(x^2-y^2)^{-3}(2x)\right]\\
&=-\frac{2}{(x^2-y^2)^2}+\frac{8x^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{-2(x^2-y^2)+8x^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{-2x^2+2y^2+8x^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{6x^2+2y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3},
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[2y(x^2-y^2)^{-2}\right]\\
&=2(x^2-y^2)^{-2}+2y\left[-2(x^2-y^2)^{-3}(-2y)\right]\\
&=\frac{2}{(x^2-y^2)^2}+\frac{8y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{2(x^2-y^2)+8y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{2x^2-2y^2+8y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{2x^2+6y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now add:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta u
&=u_{xx}+u_{yy}\\
&=\frac{6x^2+2y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}+\frac{2x^2+6y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{8x^2+8y^2}{(x^2-y^2)^3}\\
&=\frac{8(x^2+y^2)}{(x^2-y^2)^3}.
\end{aligned}
$$

This is not equal to $0$ at points of the domain. For example, at $(x,y)=(1,0)$,

$$
\Delta u(1,0)=\frac{8(1^2+0^2)}{(1^2-0^2)^3}=8\ne 0.
$$

So $u$ is not harmonic on its domain, and therefore it is harmonic on no open set at all.

##### problem 3

Consider

$$
u(x,y)=e^x\cos y.
$$

Here Theorem 1 is the natural method, because the expression is immediately recognizable as the real part of $e^z$.

Indeed,

$$
\begin{aligned}
e^z
&=e^{x+iy}\\
&=e^x e^{iy}\\
&=e^x(\cos y+i\sin y).
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\operatorname{Re}(e^z)=e^x\cos y=u(x,y).
$$

Since $e^z$ is analytic on all of $\mathbb C$, Theorem 1 implies that its real part is harmonic on all of $\mathbb C$. Hence $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

##### problem 4

Now take

$$
u(x,y)=\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}.
$$

Here Theorem 1 is more efficient than direct differentiation, because the expression is naturally related to $1/z$.

For $z=x+iy\ne 0$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{1}{z}
&=\frac{1}{x+iy}\\
&=\frac{x-iy}{(x+iy)(x-iy)}\\
&=\frac{x-iy}{x^2+y^2}\\
&=\frac{x}{x^2+y^2}-i\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\operatorname{Im}\!\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)=-\frac{y}{x^2+y^2},
$$

so

$$
u(x,y)=-\operatorname{Im}\!\left(\frac{1}{z}\right).
$$

The function $1/z$ is analytic on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}.
$$

By Theorem 1, its imaginary part is harmonic there, and multiplying by $-1$ preserves harmonicity. Therefore $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}.
$$

##### problem 5

Consider

$$
u(x,y)=\frac{1}{x+y}.
$$

Here a direct Laplacian check is the right method, because the expression depends on both $x$ and $y$ in a simple rational way, and it is not the obvious real or imaginary part of a standard analytic function.

First, the function is defined when

$$
x+y\ne 0.
$$

Now compute:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x+y)^{-1}\\
&=-1\cdot (x+y)^{-2}\cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x+y)\\
&=-(x+y)^{-2}\\
&=-\frac{1}{(x+y)^2},
\end{aligned}
$$

and similarly

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x+y)^{-1}\\
&=-1\cdot (x+y)^{-2}\cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x+y)\\
&=-(x+y)^{-2}\\
&=-\frac{1}{(x+y)^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate again:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[-(x+y)^{-2}\right]\\
&=-\left[-2(x+y)^{-3}\cdot 1\right]\\
&=2(x+y)^{-3}\\
&=\frac{2}{(x+y)^3},
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[-(x+y)^{-2}\right]\\
&=-\left[-2(x+y)^{-3}\cdot 1\right]\\
&=2(x+y)^{-3}\\
&=\frac{2}{(x+y)^3}.
\end{aligned}
$$

So

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta u
&=u_{xx}+u_{yy}\\
&=\frac{2}{(x+y)^3}+\frac{2}{(x+y)^3}\\
&=\frac{4}{(x+y)^3}.
\end{aligned}
$$

This is not zero at points of the domain. For instance, at $(0,1)$,

$$
\Delta u(0,1)=\frac{4}{(0+1)^3}=4\ne 0.
$$

Therefore $u$ is not harmonic on any open subset of its domain.

##### problem 6

Next consider

$$
u(x,y)=\sinh x\cos y.
$$

Here Theorem 1 is the natural choice, because the expression appears directly in the standard formula for $\sinh z$.

Compute:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sinh z
&=\sinh(x+iy)\\
&=\frac{e^{x+iy}-e^{-(x+iy)}}{2}\\
&=\frac{e^x(\cos y+i\sin y)-e^{-x}(\cos y-i\sin y)}{2}\\
&=\frac{(e^x-e^{-x})\cos y+i(e^x+e^{-x})\sin y}{2}\\
&=\sinh x\cos y+i\cosh x\sin y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\operatorname{Re}(\sinh z)=\sinh x\cos y=u(x,y).
$$

Since $\sinh z$ is entire, Theorem 1 gives that $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

##### problem 7

Now let

$$
u(x,y)=\cos x\cosh y.
$$

Again, Theorem 1 is the clean method, because the expression is the real part of $\cos z$.

Indeed,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\cos z
&=\cos(x+iy)\\
&=\frac{e^{i(x+iy)}+e^{-i(x+iy)}}{2}\\
&=\frac{e^{-y+ix}+e^{y-ix}}{2}\\
&=\frac{e^{-y}(\cos x+i\sin x)+e^y(\cos x-i\sin x)}{2}\\
&=\frac{(e^{-y}+e^y)\cos x+i(e^{-y}-e^y)\sin x}{2}\\
&=\cos x\cosh y-i\sin x\sinh y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\operatorname{Re}(\cos z)=\cos x\cosh y=u(x,y).
$$

Since $\cos z$ is entire, $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

##### problem 8

Consider

$$
u(x,y)=e^{2x}\cos(2y).
$$

Here Theorem 1 is preferable because the function is immediately the real part of $e^{2z}$.

We compute

$$
\begin{aligned}
e^{2z}
&=e^{2(x+iy)}\\
&=e^{2x+2iy}\\
&=e^{2x}(\cos 2y+i\sin 2y).
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence

$$
\operatorname{Re}(e^{2z})=e^{2x}\cos(2y)=u(x,y).
$$

Since $e^{2z}$ is entire, Theorem 1 gives that $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

##### problem 9

Now consider

$$
u(x,y)=e^{x^2-y^2}\cos(2xy).
$$

Here Theorem 1 is again the most efficient method, because the expression is exactly the real part of $e^{z^2}$.

First compute

$$
\begin{aligned}
z^2
&=(x+iy)^2\\
&=x^2+2ixy+i^2y^2\\
&=x^2+2ixy-y^2\\
&=x^2-y^2+2ixy.
\end{aligned}
$$

Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
e^{z^2}
&=e^{x^2-y^2+2ixy}\\
&=e^{x^2-y^2}e^{2ixy}\\
&=e^{x^2-y^2}(\cos(2xy)+i\sin(2xy)).
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\operatorname{Re}(e^{z^2})=e^{x^2-y^2}\cos(2xy)=u(x,y).
$$

Since $z^2$ is entire and the exponential function is entire, $e^{z^2}$ is entire. By Theorem 1, $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

##### problem 10

Now take

$$
u(x,y)=\ln(x^2+y^2).
$$

Here Theorem 1 is the better method because the function is closely related to $\ln|z|$, whose harmonicity was already established in the section.

For $z=x+iy\ne 0$,

$$
|z|=\sqrt{x^2+y^2}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\ln(x^2+y^2)
&=\ln\bigl(|z|^2\bigr)\\
&=2\ln|z|.
\end{aligned}
$$

The function $\ln|z|$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}.
$$

Multiplying a harmonic function by the constant $2$ preserves harmonicity. Hence $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}.
$$

It is not even defined at $z=0$, so this is the largest region on which it is harmonic.

##### problem 11

Consider

$$
u(x,y)=\ln\!\bigl((x-1)^2+y^2\bigr).
$$

Here the same method as in problem 10 is the cleanest one, because the expression is just a translated version of $\ln(x^2+y^2)$.

Observe that

$$
z-1=(x-1)+iy,
$$

so

$$
\begin{aligned}
|z-1|^2
&=((x-1)+iy)((x-1)-iy)\\
&=(x-1)^2+y^2.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\begin{aligned}
u(x,y)
&=\ln\!\bigl((x-1)^2+y^2\bigr)\\
&=\ln|z-1|^2\\
&=2\ln|z-1|.
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $\ln|w|$ is harmonic for $w\ne 0$, the translated function $\ln|z-1|$ is harmonic for

$$
z-1\ne 0,
$$

that is, for

$$
z\ne 1.
$$

Therefore $u$ is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{1\}.
$$

##### problem 12

Finally consider

$$
\arg_{\pi/2} z.
$$

Here Theorem 1, together with the discussion of branches of the logarithm and argument, is the correct method. A direct Laplacian computation would be much less natural because this is a branch-defined function, and the main issue is the domain.

The notation

$$
\arg_{\pi/2} z
$$

means the branch of the argument whose branch cut is the ray of angle

$$
\frac{\pi}{2}.
$$

That ray is

$$
\{re^{i\pi/2}:r\ge 0\}.
$$

Since

$$
e^{i\pi/2}=i,
$$

this ray is

$$
\{ir:r\ge 0\}=\{iy:y\ge 0\},
$$

which is the nonnegative imaginary axis.

On the plane with that ray removed, the branch $\arg_{\pi/2} z$ is harmonic. Therefore the function is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{iy:y\ge 0\}.
$$



> [!exercise] Exercise 9
> In Problems 13-16, find a harmonic conjugate $v(x, y)$ of the given harmonic function $u(x, y)$ by using the method of Example 2 . Check your answer by verifying the Cauchy-Riemann equations.
> 13. $x+2 y$.
> 14. $x^{2}-y^{2}-x y$.
> 15. $e^{y} \cos x$.
> 16. $\cos x \sinh y$.


##### solution

To find a harmonic conjugate $v(x,y)$ of a given harmonic function $u(x,y)$, we use the Cauchy-Riemann equations

$$
u_x=v_y,
\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

We integrate one of these equations to obtain a candidate for $v$, including an unknown function of the other variable, and then use the second equation to determine that unknown function. Finally, we verify the Cauchy-Riemann equations.

##### problem 13

Let

$$
u(x,y)=x+2y.
$$

First compute the partial derivatives of $u$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x+2y)\\
&=1,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x+2y)\\
&=2.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now use the first Cauchy-Riemann equation:

$$
v_y=u_x=1.
$$

Integrate with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
\int 1\,dy\\
&=
y+\phi(x),
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\phi(x)$ is an arbitrary function of $x$.

Next use the second Cauchy-Riemann equation:

$$
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Since $u_y=2$, this gives

$$
2=-v_x.
$$

Differentiate the expression

$$
v(x,y)=y+\phi(x)
$$

with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(y+\phi(x))\\
&=\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
2=-\phi'(x),
$$

so

$$
\phi'(x)=-2.
$$

Integrating with respect to $x$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi(x)
&=
\int (-2)\,dx\\
&=
-2x+C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Substitute back:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
y+\phi(x)\\
&=
y+(-2x+C)\\
&=
y-2x+C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now verify the Cauchy-Riemann equations:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(y-2x+C)\\
&=
0-2+0\\
&=
-2,
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(y-2x+C)\\
&=
1-0+0\\
&=
1.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
1\\
&=
u_x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
-v_x
&=
-(-2)\\
&=
2\\
&=
u_y.
\end{aligned}
$$

So one harmonic conjugate is

$$
\boxed{v(x,y)=y-2x+C.}
$$

##### problem 14

Let

$$
u(x,y)=x^2-y^2-xy.
$$

Compute the partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2-y^2-xy)\\
&=2x-y,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2-y^2-xy)\\
&=-2y-x.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now use

$$
v_y=u_x=2x-y.
$$

Integrate with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
\int (2x-y)\,dy\\
&=
\int 2x\,dy-\int y\,dy+\phi(x)\\
&=
2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+\phi(x),
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\phi(x)$ is an arbitrary function of $x$.

Now use the second Cauchy-Riemann equation:

$$
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Since

$$
u_y=-2y-x,
$$

we obtain

$$
-2y-x=-v_x.
$$

Differentiate $v$ with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+\phi(x)\right)\\
&=2y+\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Substitute into the equation:

$$
-2y-x=-(2y+\phi'(x)).
$$

Distribute the minus sign:

$$
-2y-x=-2y-\phi'(x).
$$

Add $2y$ to both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}
(-2y-x)+2y
&=
(-2y-\phi'(x))+2y\\
-x
&=
-\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Multiply by $-1$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
-1(-x)
&=
-1\bigl(-\phi'(x)\bigr)\\
x
&=
\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Integrate:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi(x)
&=
\int x\,dx\\
&=
\frac{x^2}{2}+C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+\phi(x)\\
&=
2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+\frac{x^2}{2}+C\\
&=
\frac{x^2}{2}+2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now verify. Differentiate:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{x^2}{2}+2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+C\right)\\
&=
x+2y-0+0\\
&=
x+2y,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{x^2}{2}+2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+C\right)\\
&=
0+2x-y+0\\
&=
2x-y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
2x-y\\
&=
u_x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
-v_x
&=
-(x+2y)\\
&=
-x-2y\\
&=
u_y.
\end{aligned}
$$

So one harmonic conjugate is

$$
\boxed{v(x,y)=\frac{x^2}{2}+2xy-\frac{y^2}{2}+C.}
$$

##### problem 15

Let

$$
u(x,y)=e^y\cos x.
$$

Compute the partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(e^y\cos x)\\
&=e^y(-\sin x)\\
&=-e^y\sin x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(e^y\cos x)\\
&=e^y\cos x.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now use

$$
v_y=u_x=-e^y\sin x.
$$

Integrate with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
\int (-e^y\sin x)\,dy\\
&=
-\sin x\int e^y\,dy+\phi(x)\\
&=
-\sin x\,e^y+\phi(x)\\
&=
-e^y\sin x+\phi(x),
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\phi(x)$ is an arbitrary function of $x$.

Now use

$$
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Since $u_y=e^y\cos x$, we obtain

$$
e^y\cos x=-v_x.
$$

Differentiate $v$ with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(-e^y\sin x+\phi(x)\right)\\
&=-e^y\cos x+\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
e^y\cos x=-\bigl(-e^y\cos x+\phi'(x)\bigr).
$$

Distribute the minus sign:

$$
e^y\cos x=e^y\cos x-\phi'(x).
$$

Subtract $e^y\cos x$ from both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}
e^y\cos x-e^y\cos x
&=
\bigl(e^y\cos x-\phi'(x)\bigr)-e^y\cos x\\
0
&=
-\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence

$$
\phi'(x)=0,
$$

so

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi(x)
&=
\int 0\,dx\\
&=
C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
-e^y\sin x+\phi(x)\\
&=
-e^y\sin x+C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now verify:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(-e^y\sin x+C)\\
&=
-e^y\cos x+0\\
&=
-e^y\cos x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-e^y\sin x+C)\\
&=
-e^y\sin x+0\\
&=
-e^y\sin x.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
-e^y\sin x\\
&=
u_x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
-v_x
&=
-(-e^y\cos x)\\
&=
e^y\cos x\\
&=
u_y.
\end{aligned}
$$

So one harmonic conjugate is

$$
\boxed{v(x,y)=-e^y\sin x+C.}
$$

##### problem 16

Let

$$
u(x,y)=\cos x\,\sinh y.
$$

Compute the partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(\cos x\,\sinh y)\\
&=-\sin x\,\sinh y,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(\cos x\,\sinh y)\\
&=\cos x\,\cosh y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now use

$$
v_y=u_x=-\sin x\,\sinh y.
$$

Integrate with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
\int (-\sin x\,\sinh y)\,dy\\
&=
-\sin x\int \sinh y\,dy+\phi(x)\\
&=
-\sin x\,\cosh y+\phi(x),
\end{aligned}
$$

where $\phi(x)$ is an arbitrary function of $x$.

Now impose the second Cauchy-Riemann equation:

$$
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Since

$$
u_y=\cos x\,\cosh y,
$$

we obtain

$$
\cos x\,\cosh y=-v_x.
$$

Differentiate $v$ with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(-\sin x\,\cosh y+\phi(x)\right)\\
&=-\cos x\,\cosh y+\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Hence

$$
\cos x\,\cosh y=-\bigl(-\cos x\,\cosh y+\phi'(x)\bigr).
$$

Distribute the minus sign:

$$
\cos x\,\cosh y=\cos x\,\cosh y-\phi'(x).
$$

Subtract $\cos x\,\cosh y$ from both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\cos x\,\cosh y-\cos x\,\cosh y
&=
\bigl(\cos x\,\cosh y-\phi'(x)\bigr)-\cos x\,\cosh y\\
0
&=
-\phi'(x).
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\phi'(x)=0,
$$

so

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi(x)
&=
\int 0\,dx\\
&=
C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
-\sin x\,\cosh y+\phi(x)\\
&=
-\sin x\,\cosh y+C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now verify:

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_x
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(-\sin x\,\cosh y+C)\\
&=
-\cos x\,\cosh y+0\\
&=
-\cos x\,\cosh y,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-\sin x\,\cosh y+C)\\
&=
-\sin x\,\sinh y+0\\
&=
-\sin x\,\sinh y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_y
&=
-\sin x\,\sinh y\\
&=
u_x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
-v_x
&=
-(-\cos x\,\cosh y)\\
&=
\cos x\,\cosh y\\
&=
u_y.
\end{aligned}
$$

So one harmonic conjugate is

$$
\boxed{v(x,y)=-\sin x\,\cosh y+C.}
$$


++++


> [!exercise] Exercise 10
> 17. Let $n$ be any integer. (a) Show that $u(r, \theta)=r^{n} \cos (n \theta)$ and $v(r, \theta)= r^{n} \sin (n \theta)$ are harmonic on $\mathbb{C}$ if $n \geq 0$ and on $\mathbb{C} \backslash\{0\}$ if $n<0$. (b) Find their respective harmonic conjugates. **(Hint: Consider $f(z)=z^{n}$ in polar coordinates.)**



##### solution

##### problem 17(a)

Let

$$
f(z)=z^n.
$$

Write

$$
z=re^{i\theta}.
$$

Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
f(z)
&=
z^n\\
&=
\left(re^{i\theta}\right)^n\\
&=
r^n\left(e^{i\theta}\right)^n\\
&=
r^n e^{in\theta}\\
&=
r^n\bigl(\cos(n\theta)+i\sin(n\theta)\bigr)\\
&=
r^n\cos(n\theta)+ir^n\sin(n\theta).
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Re}(z^n)
&=
r^n\cos(n\theta),
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Im}(z^n)
&=
r^n\sin(n\theta).
\end{aligned}
$$

So

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta)
$$

are the real and imaginary parts of

$$
z^n.
$$

We now determine where

$$
z^n
$$

is analytic.

First suppose that

$$
n\ge 0.
$$

If

$$
n=0,
$$

then

$$
z^n=z^0=1,
$$

which is constant and therefore analytic on all of

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

If

$$
n\ge 1,
$$

then

$$
\begin{aligned}
z^n
&=
\underbrace{z\cdot z\cdots z}_{n\text{ factors}},
\end{aligned}
$$

so

$$
z^n
$$

is a polynomial in

$$
z.
$$

Every polynomial is analytic on all of

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

Therefore, in either case

$$
n\ge 0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
z^n\text{ is analytic on }\mathbb C.
$$

By Theorem 1, the real and imaginary parts of an analytic function are harmonic on the region where the function is analytic. Hence

$$
n\ge 0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
\text{ and }
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta)
\text{ are harmonic on }\mathbb C.
$$

Now suppose that

$$
n<0.
$$

Then there exists a positive integer

$$
m
$$

such that

$$
n=-m.
$$

Substitute this into

$$
z^n
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
z^n
&=
z^{-m}\\
&=
\frac{1}{z^m}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now

$$
z^m
$$

is a polynomial, so it is analytic on

$$
\mathbb C.
$$

Also,

$$
z^m=0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
z=0.
$$

Therefore

$$
\frac{1}{z^m}
$$

is analytic precisely where

$$
z^m\neq 0,
$$

that is, on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}.
$$

Hence

$$
n<0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
z^n\text{ is analytic on }\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}.
$$

Again by Theorem 1, its real and imaginary parts are harmonic on that region. Therefore

$$
n<0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
\text{ and }
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta)
\text{ are harmonic on }\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}.
$$

Thus

$$
\boxed{
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
\quad\text{and}\quad
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta)
}
$$

are harmonic on

$$
\mathbb C
\quad\text{if }n\ge 0,
$$

and on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}
\quad\text{if }n<0.
$$

##### problem 17(b)

From part (a),

$$
\begin{aligned}
z^n
&=
r^n\cos(n\theta)+ir^n\sin(n\theta).
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore, if we write

$$
z^n=u+iv,
$$

then

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
$$

and

$$
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta).
$$

Now

$$
z^n=u+iv
$$

is analytic on the relevant domain from part (a). By definition, when

$$
f=u+iv
$$

is analytic, the function

$$
v
$$

is a harmonic conjugate of

$$
u.
$$

Hence a harmonic conjugate of

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
$$

is

$$
\boxed{v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta).}
$$

To find a harmonic conjugate of

$$
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta),
$$

use Proposition 2 from the section: if

$$
v
$$

is a harmonic conjugate of

$$
u,
$$

then

$$
-u
$$

is a harmonic conjugate of

$$
v.
$$

Since we have already shown that

$$
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta)
$$

is a harmonic conjugate of

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta),
$$

it follows that

$$
-u(r,\theta)
=
-r^n\cos(n\theta)
$$

is a harmonic conjugate of

$$
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta).
$$

Therefore the respective harmonic conjugates are

$$
\boxed{
\text{a harmonic conjugate of }r^n\cos(n\theta)\text{ is }r^n\sin(n\theta)
}
$$

and

$$
\boxed{
\text{a harmonic conjugate of }r^n\sin(n\theta)\text{ is }-r^n\cos(n\theta).
}
$$


++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 11
> 18. (a) Prove Proposition 1.
> (b) Prove Proposition 3.



##### solution

##### problem 18(a)

We prove Proposition 1.

The proposition states: if $u$ and $v$ are harmonic on an open set $S$, and $a$ and $b$ are real constants, then

$$
\phi(x,y)=a\,u(x,y)+b\,v(x,y)
$$

is harmonic on $S$.

To show that $\phi$ is harmonic, we must verify two things:

1. $\phi$ has continuous first and second partial derivatives on $S$.
2. $\Delta \phi=0$ on $S$.

Since $u$ and $v$ are harmonic on $S$, they each have continuous first and second partial derivatives on $S$. Because $\phi$ is a linear combination of $u$ and $v$ with constant coefficients, its first and second partial derivatives also exist and are continuous on $S$.

Now compute the first partial derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(a\,u+b\,v)\\
&=a\,u_x+b\,v_x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(a\,u+b\,v)\\
&=a\,u_y+b\,v_y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate again:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(a\,u_x+b\,v_x)\\
&=a\,u_{xx}+b\,v_{xx},
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
\phi_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(a\,u_y+b\,v_y)\\
&=a\,u_{yy}+b\,v_{yy}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta\phi
&=\phi_{xx}+\phi_{yy}\\
&=(a\,u_{xx}+b\,v_{xx})+(a\,u_{yy}+b\,v_{yy})\\
&=a(u_{xx}+u_{yy})+b(v_{xx}+v_{yy})\\
&=a\,\Delta u+b\,\Delta v.
\end{aligned}
$$

But $u$ and $v$ are harmonic, so

$$
\Delta u=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Delta v=0.
$$

Substituting these into the previous expression gives

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta\phi
&=a\cdot 0+b\cdot 0\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus $\phi$ satisfies Laplace's equation on $S$. Since $\phi$ also has continuous first and second partial derivatives on $S$, it follows that $\phi$ is harmonic on $S$.

Hence Proposition 1 is proved.

##### problem 18(b)

We prove Proposition 3.

The proposition states:

1. if $v_1$ and $v_2$ are harmonic conjugates of $u$ in $\Omega$, then $v_1$ and $v_2$ differ by a real constant;
2. if $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, then $v$ is also a harmonic conjugate of $u+c$ for every real constant $c$.

We prove each part separately.

##### problem 18(b)(i)

Assume that $v_1$ and $v_2$ are both harmonic conjugates of the same harmonic function $u$ in the region $\Omega$.

Then

$$
f_1=u+i v_1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
f_2=u+i v_2
$$

are analytic in $\Omega$.

Since each is analytic, the Cauchy-Riemann equations hold. For $f_1=u+i v_1$, they give

$$
u_x=(v_1)_y,
\qquad
u_y=-(v_1)_x.
$$

For $f_2=u+i v_2$, they give

$$
u_x=(v_2)_y,
\qquad
u_y=-(v_2)_x.
$$

Compare the two systems. From the first equations we obtain

$$
(v_1)_y=(v_2)_y,
$$

and from the second equations we obtain

$$
(v_1)_x=(v_2)_x.
$$

Now define

$$
w=v_1-v_2.
$$

Then

$$
\begin{aligned}
w_x
&=(v_1-v_2)_x\\
&=(v_1)_x-(v_2)_x\\
&=0,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
w_y
&=(v_1-v_2)_y\\
&=(v_1)_y-(v_2)_y\\
&=0.
\end{aligned}
$$

So both partial derivatives of $w$ are zero throughout $\Omega$.

This means that $w$ is constant on the region $\Omega$. Hence there exists a real constant $C$ such that

$$
w=C.
$$

Since $w=v_1-v_2$, we conclude that

$$
v_1-v_2=C,
$$

or equivalently,

$$
\boxed{v_1=v_2+C.}
$$

Thus any two harmonic conjugates of the same harmonic function differ by a real constant.

##### problem 18(b)(ii)

Now assume that $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$ in $\Omega$, and let $c$ be any real constant.

Because $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$, the function

$$
f=u+i v
$$

is analytic in $\Omega$. Equivalently, the Cauchy-Riemann equations hold:

$$
u_x=v_y,
\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Now consider the function

$$
\tilde u=u+c.
$$

We claim that $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $\tilde u$.

Compute the partial derivatives of $\tilde u$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\tilde u_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u+c)\\
&=u_x+0\\
&=u_x,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
\tilde u_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(u+c)\\
&=u_y+0\\
&=u_y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $u_x=v_y$ and $u_y=-v_x$, we obtain

$$
\tilde u_x=v_y
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\tilde u_y=-v_x.
$$

Thus the Cauchy-Riemann equations also hold for

$$
\tilde u+i v=(u+c)+iv.
$$

Therefore $(u+c)+iv$ is analytic in $\Omega$, which means that $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u+c$.

Hence

$$
\boxed{\text{if }v\text{ is a harmonic conjugate of }u,\text{ then }v\text{ is also a harmonic conjugate of }u+c.}
$$

This completes the proof of Proposition 3.



++++


> [!exercise] Exercise 12
> 19. Show that if $u$ and $u^{2}$ are both harmonic in a region $\Omega$, then $u$ must be constant. **(Hint: Plug $u^{2}$ into Laplace's equation and show that $u_{x}=u_{y}=0$.)**
> 20. Show that if $u, v$, and $u^{2}+v^{2}$ are harmonic in a region $\Omega$, then $u$ and $v$ must be constant. **(Hint: Plug $u^{2}+v^{2}$ into Laplace's equation and show that $u_{x}=u_{y}=0$ and $v_{x}=v_{y}=0$.)**
> 21. Suppose that $u$ is harmonic and $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$. Show that $u^{2}-v^{2}$ and $u v$ are both harmonic. **(Hint: Consider $(u+i v)^{2}$.)**




##### solution

##### problem 19

Assume that $u$ and $u^2$ are both harmonic in a region $\Omega$.

Because $u$ is harmonic, we have

$$
\Delta u=u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0
$$

throughout $\Omega$.

Because $u^2$ is also harmonic, we likewise have

$$
\Delta(u^2)=0
$$

throughout $\Omega$.

We now compute $\Delta(u^2)$ explicitly.

First differentiate with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
(u^2)_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u^2)\\
&=2u\,u_x.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate once more with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
(u^2)_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(2u\,u_x)\\
&=2u_xu_x+2u\,u_{xx}\\
&=2u_x^2+2u\,u_{xx}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now differentiate with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
(u^2)_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(u^2)\\
&=2u\,u_y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate once more with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
(u^2)_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(2u\,u_y)\\
&=2u_yu_y+2u\,u_{yy}\\
&=2u_y^2+2u\,u_{yy}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Add the two expressions:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta(u^2)
&=(u^2)_{xx}+(u^2)_{yy}\\
&=(2u_x^2+2u\,u_{xx})+(2u_y^2+2u\,u_{yy})\\
&=2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2u(u_{xx}+u_{yy})\\
&=2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2u\,\Delta u.
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $u$ is harmonic, $\Delta u=0$, so this becomes

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta(u^2)
&=2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2u\cdot 0\\
&=2u_x^2+2u_y^2.
\end{aligned}
$$

But $u^2$ is harmonic, so $\Delta(u^2)=0$. Therefore

$$
2u_x^2+2u_y^2=0.
$$

Divide by $2$:

$$
u_x^2+u_y^2=0.
$$

Now $u_x^2\ge 0$ and $u_y^2\ge 0$ at every point, since they are squares of real numbers. A sum of two nonnegative numbers can be zero only if both numbers are zero. Hence

$$
u_x=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_y=0
$$

throughout $\Omega$.

Thus all first partial derivatives of $u$ vanish on $\Omega$, so $u$ is constant on the region $\Omega$.

Therefore

$$
\boxed{u\text{ must be constant on }\Omega.}
$$

##### problem 20

Assume that $u$, $v$, and $u^2+v^2$ are all harmonic in a region $\Omega$.

Since $u$ and $v$ are harmonic, we have

$$
\Delta u=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\Delta v=0.
$$

Since $u^2+v^2$ is harmonic, we also have

$$
\Delta(u^2+v^2)=0.
$$

We compute this Laplacian term by term:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta(u^2+v^2)
&=\Delta(u^2)+\Delta(v^2).
\end{aligned}
$$

From the computation in problem 19, applied first to $u$ and then to $v$, we obtain

$$
\Delta(u^2)=2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2u\,\Delta u
$$

and

$$
\Delta(v^2)=2v_x^2+2v_y^2+2v\,\Delta v.
$$

Substitute these into the expression for $\Delta(u^2+v^2)$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta(u^2+v^2)
&=\bigl(2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2u\,\Delta u\bigr)+\bigl(2v_x^2+2v_y^2+2v\,\Delta v\bigr).
\end{aligned}
$$

Now use the fact that $\Delta u=0$ and $\Delta v=0$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta(u^2+v^2)
&=2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2u\cdot 0+2v_x^2+2v_y^2+2v\cdot 0\\
&=2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2v_x^2+2v_y^2.
\end{aligned}
$$

But $u^2+v^2$ is harmonic, so $\Delta(u^2+v^2)=0$. Hence

$$
2u_x^2+2u_y^2+2v_x^2+2v_y^2=0.
$$

Divide by $2$:

$$
u_x^2+u_y^2+v_x^2+v_y^2=0.
$$

Each term is a square of a real number, so each term is nonnegative. A sum of four nonnegative numbers can be zero only if each one is zero. Therefore

$$
u_x=0,\qquad u_y=0,\qquad v_x=0,\qquad v_y=0
$$

throughout $\Omega$.

Hence both $u$ and $v$ are constant on $\Omega$.

Therefore

$$
\boxed{u\text{ and }v\text{ must both be constant on }\Omega.}
$$

##### problem 21

Suppose that $u$ is harmonic and $v$ is a harmonic conjugate of $u$.

Then

$$
f=u+iv
$$

is analytic in the region under consideration.

The hint suggests that we consider

$$
(u+iv)^2.
$$

This is the correct method here, because if $f$ is analytic, then $f^2$ is also analytic, and Theorem 1 tells us that the real and imaginary parts of an analytic function are harmonic.

Now compute:

$$
\begin{aligned}
(u+iv)^2
&=(u+iv)(u+iv)\\
&=u^2+iuv+iuv+i^2v^2\\
&=u^2+2iuv-v^2\\
&=(u^2-v^2)+i(2uv).
\end{aligned}
$$

Since $f=u+iv$ is analytic, the function

$$
f^2=(u+iv)^2
$$

is also analytic.

Therefore, by Theorem 1, its real part and imaginary part are harmonic. The real part is

$$
u^2-v^2,
$$

and the imaginary part is

$$
2uv.
$$

Hence

$$
u^2-v^2
$$

is harmonic, and

$$
2uv
$$

is harmonic.

Finally, a constant multiple of a harmonic function is harmonic by Proposition 1. Since

$$
uv=\frac{1}{2}(2uv),
$$

it follows that $uv$ is harmonic as well.

Therefore

$$
\boxed{u^2-v^2\text{ and }uv\text{ are both harmonic.}}
$$




++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 13
> 22. Use Exercises 20 and 21 to show that if $f$ is analytic in a region $\Omega$ and $|f|$ is constant in $\Omega$, then $f$ must be constant in $\Omega$. (A different proof was outlined in Exercise 38, Section 2.4.)



##### solution

Let

$$
f(z)=u(x,y)+iv(x,y),
$$

where $u$ and $v$ are the real and imaginary parts of $f$.

Since $f$ is analytic in the region $\Omega$, Theorem 1 tells us that both $u$ and $v$ are harmonic in $\Omega$.

Now use the hypothesis that

$$
|f|
$$

is constant in $\Omega$. This means that there exists a real constant $c\ge 0$ such that

$$
|f(z)|=c
$$

for every $z\in\Omega$.

Square both sides:

$$
|f(z)|^2=c^2.
$$

But

$$
\begin{aligned}
|f(z)|^2
&=|u+iv|^2\\
&=u^2+v^2.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
u^2+v^2=c^2
$$

throughout $\Omega$.

Since $c^2$ is a constant function, it is harmonic. Thus $u^2+v^2$ is harmonic in $\Omega$.

So we now know three things:

$$
u \text{ is harmonic},\qquad v \text{ is harmonic},\qquad u^2+v^2 \text{ is harmonic}.
$$

By Exercise 20, it follows that $u$ and $v$ must both be constant on $\Omega$.

Hence

$$
f(z)=u+iv
$$

is constant on $\Omega$ as well.

Therefore

$$
\boxed{\text{if }f\text{ is analytic in }\Omega\text{ and }|f|\text{ is constant in }\Omega,\text{ then }f\text{ is constant in }\Omega.}
$$


++++


> [!exercise] Exercise 14
> 23. **Translating and dilating a harmonic function.** Suppose that $u$ is harmonic. Show that the following functions are also harmonic:
> (a) $\quad u(x-\alpha, y-\beta)$, where $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are real numbers;
> (b) $u(\alpha x, \alpha y)$, where $\alpha \neq 0$ is a real number.



##### solution

##### problem 23(a)

Assume that $u(x,y)$ is harmonic. We must show that

$$
U(x,y)=u(x-\alpha,y-\beta)
$$

is also harmonic, where $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are real constants.

Since $u$ is harmonic, it has continuous first and second partial derivatives and satisfies

$$
u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0.
$$

To simplify notation, let

$$
\xi=x-\alpha,
\qquad
\eta=y-\beta.
$$

Then

$$
U(x,y)=u(\xi,\eta).
$$

Now compute the partial derivatives of $U$ by the chain rule.

First,

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}u(\xi,\eta)\\
&=u_\xi\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial x}+u_\eta\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial x}\\
&=u_\xi(1)+u_\eta(0)\\
&=u_\xi.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate once more with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u_\xi)\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial x}+u_{\xi\eta}\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial x}\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}(1)+u_{\xi\eta}(0)\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Similarly,

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}u(\xi,\eta)\\
&=u_\xi\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial y}+u_\eta\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial y}\\
&=u_\xi(0)+u_\eta(1)\\
&=u_\eta,
\end{aligned}
$$

and therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(u_\eta)\\
&=u_{\eta\xi}\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial y}+u_{\eta\eta}\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial y}\\
&=u_{\eta\xi}(0)+u_{\eta\eta}(1)\\
&=u_{\eta\eta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now add:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta U
&=U_{xx}+U_{yy}\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}+u_{\eta\eta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

But $u$ is harmonic, so at the point $(\xi,\eta)$ we have

$$
u_{\xi\xi}+u_{\eta\eta}=0.
$$

Hence

$$
\Delta U=0.
$$

Therefore $U(x,y)=u(x-\alpha,y-\beta)$ is harmonic.

##### problem 23(b)

Now assume that $u(x,y)$ is harmonic, and define

$$
V(x,y)=u(\alpha x,\alpha y),
$$

where $\alpha\ne 0$ is a real constant.

We must show that $V$ is harmonic.

Again, since $u$ is harmonic,

$$
u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0.
$$

Let

$$
\xi=\alpha x,
\qquad
\eta=\alpha y.
$$

Then

$$
V(x,y)=u(\xi,\eta).
$$

Compute the first partial derivatives using the chain rule:

$$
\begin{aligned}
V_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}u(\xi,\eta)\\
&=u_\xi\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial x}+u_\eta\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial x}\\
&=u_\xi(\alpha)+u_\eta(0)\\
&=\alpha u_\xi,
\end{aligned}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
V_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}u(\xi,\eta)\\
&=u_\xi\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial y}+u_\eta\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial y}\\
&=u_\xi(0)+u_\eta(\alpha)\\
&=\alpha u_\eta.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate again:

$$
\begin{aligned}
V_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(\alpha u_\xi)\\
&=\alpha\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u_\xi)\\
&=\alpha\left(u_{\xi\xi}\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial x}+u_{\xi\eta}\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial x}\right)\\
&=\alpha\left(u_{\xi\xi}(\alpha)+u_{\xi\eta}(0)\right)\\
&=\alpha^2u_{\xi\xi},
\end{aligned}
$$

and similarly

$$
\begin{aligned}
V_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(\alpha u_\eta)\\
&=\alpha\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(u_\eta)\\
&=\alpha\left(u_{\eta\xi}\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial y}+u_{\eta\eta}\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial y}\right)\\
&=\alpha\left(u_{\eta\xi}(0)+u_{\eta\eta}(\alpha)\right)\\
&=\alpha^2u_{\eta\eta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now add:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta V
&=V_{xx}+V_{yy}\\
&=\alpha^2u_{\xi\xi}+\alpha^2u_{\eta\eta}\\
&=\alpha^2(u_{\xi\xi}+u_{\eta\eta}).
\end{aligned}
$$

Because $u$ is harmonic,

$$
u_{\xi\xi}+u_{\eta\eta}=0,
$$

so

$$
\Delta V=\alpha^2\cdot 0=0.
$$

Therefore $V(x,y)=u(\alpha x,\alpha y)$ is harmonic.

Hence both translated and uniformly dilated versions of a harmonic function are again harmonic.



++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 15
> 24. (a) Suppose that $u(x, y)$ is harmonic. Show that $u(x,-y)$ is also harmonic.
> 
> (b) Suppose that $u(x, y)$ is harmonic. Show that $u\left(\frac{x}{x^{2}+y^{2}}, \frac{y}{x^{2}+y^{2}}\right)$ is harmonic.
> **(Hint: Compose $u$ with the analytic function $\frac{1}{z}$; use Theorem 3 and part (a).)**
> 
> 



##### solution

##### problem 24(a)

Assume that $u(x,y)$ is harmonic. We must show that

$$
U(x,y)=u(x,-y)
$$

is also harmonic.

Since $u$ is harmonic, it has continuous first and second partial derivatives and satisfies

$$
u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0.
$$

To simplify notation, let

$$
\xi=x,
\qquad
\eta=-y.
$$

Then

$$
U(x,y)=u(\xi,\eta).
$$

Now compute the partial derivatives of $U$ by the chain rule.

First,

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_x
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}u(\xi,\eta)\\
&=u_\xi\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial x}+u_\eta\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial x}\\
&=u_\xi(1)+u_\eta(0)\\
&=u_\xi.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate once more with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_{xx}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u_\xi)\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial x}+u_{\xi\eta}\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial x}\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}(1)+u_{\xi\eta}(0)\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now compute the $y$-derivatives:

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_y
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}u(\xi,\eta)\\
&=u_\xi\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial y}+u_\eta\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial y}\\
&=u_\xi(0)+u_\eta(-1)\\
&=-u_\eta.
\end{aligned}
$$

Differentiate once more with respect to $y$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
U_{yy}
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(-u_\eta)\\
&=-\left(u_{\eta\xi}\frac{\partial \xi}{\partial y}+u_{\eta\eta}\frac{\partial \eta}{\partial y}\right)\\
&=-\left(u_{\eta\xi}(0)+u_{\eta\eta}(-1)\right)\\
&=u_{\eta\eta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now add:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta U
&=U_{xx}+U_{yy}\\
&=u_{\xi\xi}+u_{\eta\eta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

But $u$ is harmonic, so

$$
u_{\xi\xi}+u_{\eta\eta}=0.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Delta U=0.
$$

Hence

$$
\boxed{u(x,-y)\text{ is harmonic}.}
$$

##### problem 24(b)

We now show that

$$
u\left(\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}\right)
$$

is harmonic.

The hint tells us to compose $u$ with the analytic function

$$
\frac{1}{z},
$$

and then use Theorem 3 together with part (a).

First write

$$
z=x+iy.
$$

Then for $z\ne 0$,

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{1}{z}
&=\frac{1}{x+iy}\\
&=\frac{x-iy}{(x+iy)(x-iy)}\\
&=\frac{x-iy}{x^2+y^2}\\
&=\frac{x}{x^2+y^2}-i\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

So if we define

$$
f(z)=\frac{1}{z},
$$

then

$$
\operatorname{Re} f(z)=\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},
\qquad
\operatorname{Im} f(z)=-\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}.
$$

Since $f(z)=1/z$ is analytic on

$$
\mathbb C\setminus\{0\},
$$

Theorem 3 implies that the composition

$$
U(z)=u\circ f(z)
$$

is harmonic wherever it is defined. Writing this in terms of $x$ and $y$, we get

$$
\begin{aligned}
U(x,y)
&=u\!\left(\operatorname{Re}\frac{1}{z},\operatorname{Im}\frac{1}{z}\right)\\
&=u\!\left(\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},-\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}\right).
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
u\!\left(\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},-\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}\right)
$$

is harmonic for $(x,y)\ne (0,0)$.

Now apply part (a). Part (a) says that if a function $H(x,y)$ is harmonic, then

$$
H(x,-y)
$$

is also harmonic.

Take

$$
H(x,y)=u\!\left(\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},-\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}\right).
$$

Since $H$ is harmonic, part (a) implies that

$$
H(x,-y)
$$

is harmonic.

Now compute $H(x,-y)$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
H(x,-y)
&=u\!\left(\frac{x}{x^2+(-y)^2},-\frac{-y}{x^2+(-y)^2}\right)\\
&=u\!\left(\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}\right).
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{u\!\left(\frac{x}{x^2+y^2},\frac{y}{x^2+y^2}\right)\text{ is harmonic}}
$$

for all $(x,y)\ne(0,0)$ where the composition is defined.



++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 16
> 
> 
> 25. **Harmonic functions independent of $y$.** 
> (a) Suppose that $u(x, y)$ is a harmonic function whose values depend only on $x$ and not on $y$. Using Laplace's equation, show that $u(x, y)=a x+b$, where $a$ and $b$ are real constants.
> (b) Consider the Dirichlet problem in the infinite vertical strip in _Figure 14_. Because the boundary values do not depend on $y$, it is plausible to try for a solution a harmonic function whose values do not depend on $y$. Solve the problem, using (a).
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 14
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-50_493_558_342_84.jpg)
> > Dirichlet problem in problem 25.
> 
> 
> (c) Determine and plot the isotherms and curves of heat flow.
> 
> 



##### solution

##### problem 25(a)

Suppose that $u(x,y)$ is harmonic and depends only on $x$ and not on $y$. Then there is a one-variable function, which we still denote by $u$, such that

$$
u(x,y)=u(x).
$$

Because $u$ is independent of $y$, we have

$$
u_y=0.
$$

Differentiate once more with respect to $y$:

$$
u_{yy}=0.
$$

Since $u$ is harmonic, it satisfies Laplace's equation

$$
u_{xx}+u_{yy}=0.
$$

Substitute $u_{yy}=0$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{xx}+u_{yy}
&=
0\\
u_{xx}+0
&=
0.
\end{aligned}
$$

So

$$
u_{xx}=0.
$$

Now integrate with respect to $x$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=
\int 0\,dx\\
&=
a,
\end{aligned}
$$

where $a$ is a real constant. Integrating again,

$$
\begin{aligned}
u(x)
&=
\int (a)\,dx\\
&=
ax+b,
\end{aligned}
$$

where $b$ is another real constant.

Therefore

$$
\boxed{u(x,y)=ax+b}
$$

for some real constants $a$ and $b$.

##### problem 25(b)

We now solve the Dirichlet problem in Figure 14. The region is the infinite vertical strip

$$
3<x<5,
\qquad -\infty<y<\infty,
$$

with boundary conditions

$$
u(3,y)=10
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u(5,y)=40.
$$

Because the boundary values do not depend on $y$, part (a) suggests trying a solution of the form

$$
u(x,y)=ax+b.
$$

Now impose the boundary conditions.

At

$$
x=3,
$$

we must have

$$
u(3,y)=3a+b=10.
$$

At

$$
x=5,
$$

we must have

$$
u(5,y)=5a+b=40.
$$

We now solve the system

$$
3a+b=10,
\qquad
5a+b=40.
$$

Subtract the first equation from the second:

$$
(5a+b)-(3a+b)=40-10.
$$

Simplify:

$$
2a=30.
$$

Hence

$$
a=15.
$$

Substitute into

$$
3a+b=10
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
3a+b
&=
10\\
3(15)+b
&=
10\\
45+b
&=
10.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
b
&=
10-45\\
&=
-35.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus the solution is

$$
\boxed{u(x,y)=15x-35.}
$$

We can check the boundary values directly:

$$
u(3,y)=15(3)-35=45-35=10,
$$

and

$$
u(5,y)=15(5)-35=75-35=40.
$$

So this function satisfies the boundary conditions, and since it is linear in $x$, it is harmonic by part (a).

##### problem 25(c)

The isotherms are the level curves of the temperature function

$$
u(x,y)=15x-35.
$$

Set

$$
u(x,y)=T,
$$

where

$$
10<T<40.
$$

Then

$$
15x-35=T.
$$

Add $35$ to both sides:

$$
15x=T+35.
$$

Divide by $15$:

$$
x=\frac{T+35}{15}.
$$

So each isotherm is a vertical line

$$
\boxed{x=\frac{T+35}{15}},
\qquad 10<T<40.
$$

As $T$ increases from $10$ to $40$, these vertical lines sweep from the left boundary

$$
x=3
$$

to the right boundary

$$
x=5.
$$

Now find the curves of heat flow.

First compute a harmonic conjugate $v(x,y)$ of $u(x,y)=15x-35$. The constant $-35$ does not affect the derivatives, so we use the Cauchy-Riemann equations:

$$
u_x=v_y,
\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Since

$$
u_x=15
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_y=0,
$$

we get

$$
v_y=15
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v_x=0.
$$

Integrating

$$
v_y=15
$$

with respect to $y$, we obtain

$$
\begin{aligned}
v(x,y)
&=
\int 15\,dy\\
&=
15y+C,
\end{aligned}
$$

where $C$ is a real constant. This already satisfies

$$
v_x=0,
$$

so it is a harmonic conjugate.

The curves of heat flow are the level curves of $v$:

$$
v(x,y)=K.
$$

Thus

$$
15y+C=K.
$$

Subtract $C$ from both sides:

$$
\begin{aligned}
15y+C
&=
K\\
15y
&=
K-C.
\end{aligned}
$$

Divide by $15$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
y
&=
\frac{K-C}{15}.
\end{aligned}
$$

So the curves of heat flow are the horizontal lines

$$
\boxed{y=c},
\qquad c\in\mathbb R.
$$

Since the left boundary is at temperature $10^\circ$ and the right boundary is at temperature $40^\circ$, heat flows from right to left, that is, in the direction of decreasing temperature. This agrees with

$$
\nabla u=(15,0),
$$

so

$$
-\nabla u=(-15,0),
$$

which points to the left.

Therefore the geometric picture is:

1. isotherms: vertical lines;
2. curves of heat flow: horizontal lines;
3. heat flows leftward from the hotter side $u=40^\circ$ to the cooler side $u=10^\circ$.

++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 17
> 
> 26. **A Dirichlet problem in an infinite vertical strip.** 
> (a) Solve the Dirichlet problem in _Figure 15_.
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 15
> > 
> > ![[Pasted image 20260401005538.png|300]]
> > Dirichlet problem in Problem 26.
> 
> 
> (b) Determine and plot the isotherms and curves of heat flow.
> 
> 




##### solution

##### problem 26(a)

We solve the Dirichlet problem in the infinite vertical strip shown in Figure 15. The region is

$$
-3<x<3,
\qquad
-\infty<y<\infty,
$$

with boundary conditions

$$
u(-3,y)=70
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u(3,y)=-20.
$$

Because the boundary values do not depend on $y$, it is natural to try a harmonic function that depends only on $x$. By Exercise 25(a), every harmonic function independent of $y$ has the form

$$
u(x,y)=ax+b,
$$

where $a$ and $b$ are real constants.

Now impose the boundary conditions.

At

$$
x=-3,
$$

we must have

$$
u(-3,y)=-3a+b=70.
$$

At

$$
x=3,
$$

we must have

$$
u(3,y)=3a+b=-20.
$$

So we must solve the system

$$
-3a+b=70,
\qquad
3a+b=-20.
$$

Subtract the first equation from the second:

$$
(3a+b)-(-3a+b)=-20-70.
$$

Simplify:

$$
6a=-90.
$$

Hence

$$
a=-15.
$$

Substitute this into

$$
3a+b=-20:
$$

$$
3(-15)+b=-20.
$$

Thus

$$
-45+b=-20.
$$

Add $45$ to both sides:

$$
b=25.
$$

Therefore the solution is

$$
\boxed{u(x,y)=-15x+25.}
$$

We check the boundary values:

$$
u(-3,y)=-15(-3)+25=45+25=70,
$$

and

$$
u(3,y)=-15(3)+25=-45+25=-20.
$$

So this function satisfies the required boundary conditions.

##### problem 26(b)

The isotherms are the level curves of

$$
u(x,y)=-15x+25.
$$

Set

$$
u(x,y)=T.
$$

Then

$$
-15x+25=T.
$$

Subtract $25$ from both sides:

$$
-15x=T-25.
$$

Multiply both sides by $-1$:

$$
15x=25-T.
$$

Divide by $15$:

$$
x=\frac{25-T}{15}.
$$

Thus each isotherm is a vertical line

$$
\boxed{x=\frac{25-T}{15}}.
$$

As $T$ decreases from $70$ to $-20$, these lines move from the left boundary

$$
x=-3
$$

to the right boundary

$$
x=3.
$$

Now find the curves of heat flow.

We compute a harmonic conjugate $v(x,y)$ of

$$
u(x,y)=-15x+25.
$$

The constant $25$ does not affect the derivatives, so the Cauchy-Riemann equations give

$$
u_x=v_y,
\qquad
u_y=-v_x.
$$

Since

$$
u_x=-15
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_y=0,
$$

we obtain

$$
v_y=-15
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
v_x=0.
$$

Integrating

$$
v_y=-15
$$

with respect to $y$, we get

$$
v(x,y)=-15y+C,
$$

where $C$ is a real constant. This already satisfies

$$
v_x=0,
$$

so it is a harmonic conjugate.

The curves of heat flow are the level curves of $v$:

$$
v(x,y)=K.
$$

Thus

$$
-15y+C=K.
$$

Absorbing constants into a new constant gives

$$
y=\text{constant}.
$$

Therefore the curves of heat flow are the horizontal lines

$$
\boxed{y=c},
\qquad c\in\mathbb R.
$$

To determine the direction of heat flow, compute the gradient:

$$
\nabla u=(u_x,u_y)=(-15,0).
$$

Heat flows in the direction of

$$
-\nabla u=(15,0),
$$

which points to the right. This is physically correct: heat flows from the hotter left boundary $u=70^\circ$ toward the colder right boundary $u=-20^\circ$.

So the geometric picture is:

1. isotherms: vertical lines;
2. curves of heat flow: horizontal lines;
3. heat flows rightward.

++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 18
> 
> 27. Harmonic functions independent of $r$. 
> (a) Solve the Dirichlet problem in _Figure 16_. Because the boundary values do not depend on $r$, you should try for a solution a harmonic function whose values do not depend on $r$.
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 16
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-50_495_535_1109_852.jpg)
> > Dirichlet problem in Exercise 27.
> 
> 
> (b) Determine and plot the isotherms and curves of heat flow.
> 
> 



##### solution

##### problem 27(a)

The region in Figure 16 is the wedge between the two rays

$$
\theta=\frac{5\pi}{6}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\theta=\frac{7\pi}{6},
$$

with boundary values

$$
u=150
\quad\text{on}\quad
\theta=\frac{5\pi}{6},
$$

and

$$
u=50
\quad\text{on}\quad
\theta=\frac{7\pi}{6}.
$$

Because the boundary values are independent of $r$, we try a harmonic function that depends only on the angle. On this wedge we use the branch

$$
\theta=\arg_{5\pi/6} z,
$$

so that throughout the wedge

$$
\frac{5\pi}{6}<\theta<\frac{7\pi}{6}.
$$

As in the discussion earlier in the section, a harmonic function independent of $r$ must have the form

$$
u=a\theta+b.
$$

So we write

$$
u(r,\theta)=a\theta+b.
$$

Now impose the boundary conditions.

On the upper ray,

$$
\theta=\frac{5\pi}{6},
$$

so

$$
a\frac{5\pi}{6}+b=150.
$$

On the lower ray,

$$
\theta=\frac{7\pi}{6},
$$

so

$$
a\frac{7\pi}{6}+b=50.
$$

We now solve the system

$$
a\frac{5\pi}{6}+b=150,
\qquad
a\frac{7\pi}{6}+b=50.
$$

Subtract the first equation from the second:

$$
a\frac{7\pi}{6}-a\frac{5\pi}{6}=50-150.
$$

Simplify:

$$
a\left(\frac{2\pi}{6}\right)=-100.
$$

Since

$$
\frac{2\pi}{6}=\frac{\pi}{3},
$$

we get

$$
a\frac{\pi}{3}=-100.
$$

Therefore

$$
a=-\frac{300}{\pi}.
$$

Substitute this into

$$
a\frac{5\pi}{6}+b=150
$$

to get

$$
\left(-\frac{300}{\pi}\right)\frac{5\pi}{6}+b=150.
$$

Simplify:

$$
-300\cdot\frac{5}{6}+b=150,
$$

so

$$
-250+b=150.
$$

Hence

$$
b=400.
$$

Thus the solution is

$$
\boxed{u(r,\theta)=400-\frac{300}{\pi}\theta},
\qquad
\frac{5\pi}{6}<\theta<\frac{7\pi}{6},
$$

where

$$
\theta=\arg_{5\pi/6} z.
$$

Equivalently,

$$
\boxed{u(z)=400-\frac{300}{\pi}\arg_{5\pi/6} z.}
$$

We check the boundary values:

$$
u\left(r,\frac{5\pi}{6}\right)=400-\frac{300}{\pi}\cdot\frac{5\pi}{6}=400-250=150,
$$

and

$$
u\left(r,\frac{7\pi}{6}\right)=400-\frac{300}{\pi}\cdot\frac{7\pi}{6}=400-350=50.
$$

So the function satisfies the required boundary conditions.

##### problem 27(b)

The isotherms are the level curves of

$$
u(r,\theta)=400-\frac{300}{\pi}\theta.
$$

Set

$$
u(r,\theta)=T,
$$

where

$$
50<T<150.
$$

Then

$$
400-\frac{300}{\pi}\theta=T.
$$

Subtract $400$ from both sides:

$$
-\frac{300}{\pi}\theta=T-400.
$$

Multiply both sides by

$$
-\frac{\pi}{300}
$$

to get

$$
\theta=\frac{\pi}{300}(400-T).
$$

So each isotherm is a ray from the origin:

$$
\boxed{\theta=\frac{\pi}{300}(400-T)},
\qquad 50<T<150.
$$

As $T$ decreases from $150$ to $50$, the angle increases from

$$
\frac{5\pi}{6}
$$

to

$$
\frac{7\pi}{6}.
$$

Thus the isotherms sweep through the wedge from the upper boundary ray to the lower boundary ray.

Now find the curves of heat flow.

Since

$$
u(z)=400-\frac{300}{\pi}\arg_{5\pi/6} z,
$$

and $\arg z$ is a harmonic conjugate of $-\ln|z|$, a harmonic conjugate of $u$ is

$$
v(z)=\frac{300}{\pi}\ln|z|,
$$

up to an additive real constant.

The curves of heat flow are the level curves of $v$, so set

$$
v(z)=C.
$$

Then

$$
\frac{300}{\pi}\ln|z|=C.
$$

Multiply both sides by

$$
\frac{\pi}{300}
$$

to get

$$
\ln|z|=\frac{\pi C}{300}.
$$

Exponentiate:

$$
|z|=e^{\pi C/300}.
$$

Since the right-hand side is a positive constant, we may write

$$
|z|=R,
\qquad R>0.
$$

Therefore the curves of heat flow are circles centered at the origin. Because the region is only the wedge, the relevant portions are the circular arcs

$$
\boxed{r=R},
\qquad R>0,
\qquad
\frac{5\pi}{6}<\theta<\frac{7\pi}{6}.
$$

To determine the direction of heat flow, note that the temperature decreases from $150^\circ$ on the upper ray to $50^\circ$ on the lower ray. Hence heat flows from the upper boundary ray toward the lower boundary ray. Along the circular arcs this means the flow is clockwise.

So the geometric picture is:

1. isotherms: rays from the origin;
2. curves of heat flow: circular arcs centered at the origin;
3. heat flows clockwise from the $150^\circ$ boundary toward the $50^\circ$ boundary.



![|center|500](Pasted%20image%2020260425010715.png)

  {GrayLevel[0.35], Dashed, Thick,
   Table[
    Line[{{0, 0}, 4.3 {Cos[th], Sin[th]}}],
    {th, 5 Pi/6 + Pi/18, 7 Pi/6 - Pi/18, Pi/18}
    ]},
  
  {Black, Thick,
   Table[
    Circle[{0, 0}, r, {5 Pi/6, 7 Pi/6}],

  },

]

++++




> [!exercise] Exercise 19
> 
> 
> 28. A Dirichlet problem in a wedge. 
> (a) Solve the Dirichlet problem in _Figure 17_.
> > [!figure] Figure 17
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-50_498_503_1097_1592.jpg)
> > Dirichlet problem in Exercise 28.
> 
> (b) Determine and plot the isotherms and curves of heat flow.




##### solution

##### problem 28(a)

The region in Figure 17 is the wedge between the two rays

$$
\theta=\frac{3\pi}{5}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\theta=\frac{9\pi}{10}.
$$

Indeed, the upper ray is obtained by moving an angle

$$
\frac{\pi}{10}
$$

to the left of the positive $y$-axis, so its angle is

$$
\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{\pi}{10}
=\frac{5\pi}{10}+\frac{\pi}{10}
=\frac{6\pi}{10}
=\frac{3\pi}{5},
$$

and the lower ray is obtained by moving an angle

$$
\frac{\pi}{10}
$$

above the negative $x$-axis, so its angle is

$$
\pi-\frac{\pi}{10}
=\frac{10\pi}{10}-\frac{\pi}{10}
=\frac{9\pi}{10}.
$$

The boundary values are

$$
u=0
\quad\text{on}\quad
\theta=\frac{3\pi}{5},
$$

and

$$
u=60
\quad\text{on}\quad
\theta=\frac{9\pi}{10}.
$$

Because the boundary values do not depend on $r$, we try a harmonic function that depends only on the angle. On this wedge we use the branch

$$
\theta=\arg_{3\pi/5} z,
$$

so that throughout the region

$$
\frac{3\pi}{5}<\theta<\frac{9\pi}{10}.
$$

As in the preceding exercise, a harmonic function independent of $r$ must have the form

$$
u=a\theta+b.
$$

So we write

$$
u(r,\theta)=a\theta+b.
$$

Now impose the boundary conditions.

On the ray

$$
\theta=\frac{3\pi}{5},
$$

we must have

$$
a\frac{3\pi}{5}+b=0.
$$

On the ray

$$
\theta=\frac{9\pi}{10},
$$

we must have

$$
a\frac{9\pi}{10}+b=60.
$$

Therefore $a$ and $b$ satisfy the system

$$
a\frac{3\pi}{5}+b=0,
\qquad
a\frac{9\pi}{10}+b=60.
$$

Subtract the first equation from the second:

$$
a\frac{9\pi}{10}-a\frac{3\pi}{5}=60-0.
$$

Factor out $a$:

$$
a\left(\frac{9\pi}{10}-\frac{3\pi}{5}\right)=60.
$$

Rewrite

$$
\frac{3\pi}{5}
=\frac{6\pi}{10},
$$

so

$$
a\left(\frac{9\pi}{10}-\frac{6\pi}{10}\right)=60.
$$

Subtract inside the parentheses:

$$
a\left(\frac{3\pi}{10}\right)=60.
$$

Solve for $a$:

$$
a
=60\cdot\frac{10}{3\pi}
=\frac{600}{3\pi}
=\frac{200}{\pi}.
$$

Now substitute this into

$$
a\frac{3\pi}{5}+b=0
$$

to get

$$
\left(\frac{200}{\pi}\right)\frac{3\pi}{5}+b=0.
$$

Simplify:

$$
200\cdot\frac{3}{5}+b=0,
$$

so

$$
120+b=0.
$$

Hence

$$
b=-120.
$$

Therefore the solution is

$$
\boxed{u(r,\theta)=\frac{200}{\pi}\theta-120},
\qquad
\frac{3\pi}{5}<\theta<\frac{9\pi}{10}.
$$

Equivalently,

$$
\boxed{u(z)=\frac{200}{\pi}\arg_{3\pi/5} z-120.}
$$

We check the boundary values explicitly:

$$
u\left(r,\frac{3\pi}{5}\right)
=\frac{200}{\pi}\cdot\frac{3\pi}{5}-120
=200\cdot\frac{3}{5}-120
=120-120
=0,
$$

and

$$
u\left(r,\frac{9\pi}{10}\right)
=\frac{200}{\pi}\cdot\frac{9\pi}{10}-120
=200\cdot\frac{9}{10}-120
=180-120
=60.
$$

So the function satisfies the required boundary conditions.

##### problem 28(b)

The isotherms are the level curves of

$$
u(r,\theta)=\frac{200}{\pi}\theta-120.
$$

Set

$$
u(r,\theta)=T,
$$

where

$$
0<T<60.
$$

Then

$$
\frac{200}{\pi}\theta-120=T.
$$

Add $120$ to both sides:

$$
\frac{200}{\pi}\theta=T+120.
$$

Multiply both sides by

$$
\frac{\pi}{200}
$$

to get

$$
\theta=\frac{\pi}{200}(T+120).
$$

Therefore each isotherm is a ray from the origin:

$$
\boxed{\theta=\frac{\pi}{200}(T+120)},
\qquad 0<T<60.
$$

As $T$ increases from $0$ to $60$, the angle increases from

$$
\frac{\pi}{200}(0+120)
=\frac{120\pi}{200}
=\frac{3\pi}{5}
$$

to

$$
\frac{\pi}{200}(60+120)
=\frac{180\pi}{200}
=\frac{9\pi}{10}.
$$

So the isotherms sweep through the wedge from the $0^\circ$ boundary ray to the $60^\circ$ boundary ray.

Now determine the curves of heat flow.

Since

$$
\operatorname{Log} z=\ln|z|+i\arg z,
$$

we have

$$
-i\operatorname{Log} z
=-i\ln|z|-i(i\arg z)
=-i\ln|z|+\arg z.
$$

Therefore

$$
\Re(-i\operatorname{Log} z)=\arg z.
$$

It follows that

$$
\Re\left(\frac{200}{\pi}(-i\operatorname{Log} z)-120\right)
=\frac{200}{\pi}\arg z-120
=u(z).
$$

So a harmonic conjugate of $u$ is

$$
v(z)=-\frac{200}{\pi}\ln|z|,
$$

up to an additive real constant.

The curves of heat flow are the level curves of $v$, so set

$$
v(z)=C.
$$

Then

$$
-\frac{200}{\pi}\ln|z|=C.
$$

Multiply both sides by

$$
-\frac{\pi}{200}
$$

to get

$$
\ln|z|=-\frac{\pi C}{200}.
$$

Exponentiate:

$$
|z|=e^{-\pi C/200}.
$$

Since the right-hand side is a positive constant, we may write

$$
|z|=R,
\qquad R>0.
$$

Therefore the curves of heat flow are circles centered at the origin. Since the region is only the wedge, the relevant parts are the circular arcs

$$
\boxed{r=R},
\qquad R>0,
\qquad
\frac{3\pi}{5}<\theta<\frac{9\pi}{10}.
$$

To determine the direction of heat flow, note that the temperature is $60^\circ$ on the lower ray and $0^\circ$ on the upper ray. Hence heat flows from the lower boundary ray toward the upper boundary ray. Along the circular arcs this means the flow is counterclockwise.

Thus the picture is the following: the isotherms are rays from the origin, the heat-flow curves are circular arcs centered at the origin, and the arrows on the heat-flow curves point counterclockwise from the $60^\circ$ side toward the $0^\circ$ side.

> [!exercise] Exercise 20
> 29. Harmonic functions independent of $\theta$. Suppose that $u(r, \theta)$ is a harmonic function, in polar coordinates.
> (a) Suppose that $u$ depends only on $r$ and not $\theta$; hence $u(r, \theta)=u(r)$. Using the polar form of the Laplacian (12) in Exercise 47, show that $u(r)$ satisfies the (ordinary) differential equation in $r$
> 
> $$
> u_{r r}+\frac{1}{r} u_{r}=0,
> $$
> 
> known as an Euler equation. This is perhaps the simplest second order linear differential equation with nonconstant coefficients. To find its general solution, we
> (b) Multiply the Euler equation by the integrating factor $r$ and notice that the need two linearly independent solutions. left side is now exact. Integrate to conclude $r u_{r}=c_{1}$, where $c_{1}$ is the constant of
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 21
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-51_470_496_1775_89.jpg)
> > Figure 21 Dirichlet problem for problem 35.
> 
> integration. Integrate again and find the general solution
> 
> 
> 
> $$
> u(r)=c_{1} \ln r+c_{2} .
> $$
> 



##### solution

##### problem 29(a)

We are told that $u(r,\theta)$ is harmonic and that $u$ depends only on $r$. Thus

$$
u(r,\theta)=u(r).
$$

The polar form of Laplace's equation is

$$
u_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}u_r+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}=0.
$$

Because $u$ does not depend on $\theta$, its derivative with respect to $\theta$ is zero:

$$
u_\theta=0.
$$

Differentiate once more with respect to $\theta$:

$$
u_{\theta\theta}=0.
$$

Substitute this into the polar Laplace equation:

$$
u_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}u_r+\frac{1}{r^2}(0)=0.
$$

Simplify:

$$
u_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}u_r=0.
$$

This is the required ordinary differential equation.

##### problem 29(b)

We now solve

$$
u_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}u_r=0.
$$

Multiply both sides by the integrating factor $r$:

$$
r\left(u_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}u_r\right)=r\cdot 0.
$$

Distribute $r$ on the left-hand side:

$$
ru_{rr}+r\cdot\frac{1}{r}u_r=0.
$$

Simplify $r\cdot \dfrac{1}{r}=1$:

$$
ru_{rr}+u_r=0.
$$

Now observe that

$$
\frac{d}{dr}(ru_r)=1\cdot u_r+r\cdot u_{rr}=u_r+ru_{rr}.
$$

Since addition is commutative,

$$
u_r+ru_{rr}=ru_{rr}+u_r.
$$

Therefore

$$
\frac{d}{dr}(ru_r)=ru_{rr}+u_r.
$$

So the differential equation becomes

$$
\frac{d}{dr}(ru_r)=0.
$$

Integrate with respect to $r$:

$$
ru_r=c_1,
$$

where $c_1$ is a constant of integration.

Now solve for $u_r$:

$$
u_r=\frac{c_1}{r}.
$$

Integrate again with respect to $r$:

$$
u(r)=\int \frac{c_1}{r}\,dr.
$$

Factor out the constant $c_1$:

$$
u(r)=c_1\int \frac{1}{r}\,dr.
$$

Use

$$
\int \frac{1}{r}\,dr=\ln r+c,
$$

for $r>0$, to obtain

$$
u(r)=c_1\ln r+c_2,
$$

where $c_2$ is another constant of integration.

Therefore the general solution is

$$
\boxed{u(r)=c_1\ln r+c_2.}
$$



++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 21
> 
> 30. Dirichlet problems in annular regions. The annular region $A_{R_{1}, R_{2}}$ in _Figure 18_ is centered at the origin with inner radius $R_{1}$ and outer radius $R_{2}$. Consider the Dirichlet problem in $A_{R_{1}, R_{2}}$ with constant boundary conditions $u\left(R_{1}, \theta\right)= T_{1}$ and $u\left(R_{2}, \theta\right)=T_{2}$ for all $\theta$. Show that the solution of the problem is
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 18
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-51_461_492_971_97.jpg)
> > Figure 18 Dirichlet problem in problem 30.
> 
> 
> $$
> u(r, \theta)=u(r)=T_{1}+\left(T_{2}-T_{1}\right) \frac{\ln \left(r / R_{1}\right)}{\ln \left(R_{2} / R_{1}\right)}
> $$
> 
> **(Hint: Since the boundary conditions are independent of $\theta$, you should try a harmonic function independent of $\theta$. According to Problem 29, try $u(r)=c_{1} \ln r+c_{2}$.)** 



##### solution

Since the boundary conditions are independent of $\theta$, it is natural to look for a harmonic function that also does not depend on $\theta$. By Problem 29, every harmonic function of this type has the form

$$
u(r,\theta)=u(r)=c_1\ln r+c_2.
$$

We now determine the constants $c_1$ and $c_2$ from the boundary conditions.

On the inner circle

$$
r=R_1,
$$

we are given

$$
u(R_1,\theta)=T_1.
$$

Substitute $r=R_1$ into

$$
u(r)=c_1\ln r+c_2
$$

to get

$$
c_1\ln R_1+c_2=T_1.
$$

On the outer circle

$$
r=R_2,
$$

we are given

$$
u(R_2,\theta)=T_2.
$$

Substitute $r=R_2$ into

$$
u(r)=c_1\ln r+c_2
$$

to get

$$
c_1\ln R_2+c_2=T_2.
$$

Thus $c_1$ and $c_2$ satisfy the system

$$
c_1\ln R_1+c_2=T_1,
\qquad
c_1\ln R_2+c_2=T_2.
$$

Subtract the first equation from the second:

$$
c_1\ln R_2-c_1\ln R_1=T_2-T_1.
$$

Factor out $c_1$:

$$
c_1(\ln R_2-\ln R_1)=T_2-T_1.
$$

Use the logarithm identity

$$
\ln R_2-\ln R_1=\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)
$$

to obtain

$$
c_1\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)=T_2-T_1.
$$

Therefore

$$
c_1=\frac{T_2-T_1}{\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)}.
$$

Now solve for $c_2$. From

$$
c_1\ln R_1+c_2=T_1
$$

we get

$$
c_2=T_1-c_1\ln R_1.
$$

Substitute the value of $c_1$:

$$
c_2=T_1-\frac{T_2-T_1}{\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)}\ln R_1.
$$

Now substitute $c_1$ and $c_2$ back into

$$
u(r)=c_1\ln r+c_2.
$$

This gives

$$
u(r)=\frac{T_2-T_1}{\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)}\ln r
+T_1-\frac{T_2-T_1}{\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)}\ln R_1.
$$

Group the terms involving the common factor

$$
\frac{T_2-T_1}{\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)}
$$

$$
u(r)=T_1+\frac{T_2-T_1}{\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)}(\ln r-\ln R_1).
$$

Use the logarithm identity

$$
\ln r-\ln R_1=\ln\left(\frac{r}{R_1}\right)
$$

to obtain

$$
u(r)=T_1+\frac{T_2-T_1}{\ln\left(\frac{R_2}{R_1}\right)}\ln\left(\frac{r}{R_1}\right).
$$

Therefore the solution is

$$
\boxed{
u(r,\theta)=u(r)=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{\ln\left(r/R_1\right)}{\ln\left(R_2/R_1\right)}
}.
$$

We can check the boundary values directly. If $r=R_1$, then

$$
u(R_1,\theta)
=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{\ln\left(R_1/R_1\right)}{\ln\left(R_2/R_1\right)}
=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{\ln 1}{\ln\left(R_2/R_1\right)}
=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{0}{\ln\left(R_2/R_1\right)}
=T_1.
$$

If $r=R_2$, then

$$
u(R_2,\theta)
=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{\ln\left(R_2/R_1\right)}{\ln\left(R_2/R_1\right)}
=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)
=T_2.
$$

So the formula satisfies both boundary conditions.


++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 22
> 
> 
> Problems 31-32 are Dirichlet problems described by the corresponding figures. In each case, (a) solve the Dirichlet problem.
> (b) Determine the isotherms.
> (c) Determine the curves of heat flow. (d) Plot the isotherms and curves of heat flow.
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 19
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-51_457_500_979_802.jpg)
> > 
> > Dirichlet problem in problem 31.
> 
> 
> 
> 
> > [!figure] Figure 20
> > 
> > ![300](./images/af4f9593-dec7-4bc0-8d78-d25500419ec9-51_461_508_981_1511.jpg)
> > 
> > Dirichlet problem in problem 32.
> 
> 
> 
> 




##### solution

For both problems, the boundary conditions are constant on circles centered at the origin, so they do not depend on $\theta$. For that reason, the correct method is to use the radial solution from Exercise 30:

$$
u(r,\theta)=u(r)=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{\ln(r/R_1)}{\ln(R_2/R_1)}.
$$

Once $u(r)$ is known, the isotherms are the level curves of $u$, so they are circles

$$
r=\text{constant},
$$

and a harmonic conjugate is a constant multiple of $\theta$, so the curves of heat flow are rays

$$
\theta=\text{constant}.
$$

##### problem 31(a)

In Figure 19 the annulus is

$$
A_{1,2},
$$

so

$$
R_1=1,
\qquad
R_2=2.
$$

The boundary values are

$$
u(1,\theta)=0,
\qquad
u(2,\theta)=100.
$$

Thus

$$
T_1=0,
\qquad
T_2=100.
$$

Substitute these values into the formula from Exercise 30:

$$
u(r)=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{\ln(r/R_1)}{\ln(R_2/R_1)}.
$$

This gives

$$
u(r)=0+\left(100-0\right)\frac{\ln(r/1)}{\ln(2/1)}.
$$

Simplify:

$$
u(r)=100\frac{\ln r}{\ln 2}.
$$

Therefore the solution is

$$
\boxed{u(r,\theta)=u(r)=100\frac{\ln r}{\ln 2}},
\qquad 1<r<2.
$$

We can check the boundary values directly. If $r=1$, then

$$
u(1,\theta)=100\frac{\ln 1}{\ln 2}=100\frac{0}{\ln 2}=0.
$$

If $r=2$, then

$$
u(2,\theta)=100\frac{\ln 2}{\ln 2}=100.
$$

So the formula satisfies the required boundary conditions.

##### problem 31(b)

The isotherms are the level curves of

$$
u(r)=100\frac{\ln r}{\ln 2}.
$$

Set

$$
u(r)=T,
$$

where

$$
0<T<100.
$$

Then

$$
100\frac{\ln r}{\ln 2}=T.
$$

Multiply both sides by

$$
\frac{\ln 2}{100}
$$

to get

$$
\ln r=\frac{T\ln 2}{100}.
$$

Exponentiate:

$$
r=e^{(T\ln 2)/100}.
$$

Use

$$
e^{a\ln 2}=2^a
$$

to obtain

$$
\boxed{r=2^{T/100}},
\qquad 0<T<100.
$$

Therefore the isotherms are concentric circles centered at the origin.

##### problem 31(c)

The isotherms found in part (b) are the circles

$$
r=\text{constant}.
$$

The curves of heat flow must be orthogonal to the isotherms. A ray

$$
\theta=\theta_0
$$

meets every circle

$$
r=\text{constant}
$$

at a right angle, so the orthogonal trajectories of the isotherms are precisely the rays from the origin.

If we want to see this analytically, we may remove one ray from the annulus and choose a branch of $\operatorname{Log} z$ on the resulting slit annulus. On that slit annulus,

$$
f(z)=100\frac{\operatorname{Log} z}{\ln 2}
$$

has real part

$$
\Re f(z)=100\frac{\ln|z|}{\ln 2}=100\frac{\ln r}{\ln 2}=u(r),
$$

so a local harmonic conjugate is

$$
v(r,\theta)=100\frac{\theta}{\ln 2},
$$

up to an additive constant. Its level curves satisfy

$$
100\frac{\theta}{\ln 2}=C,
$$

hence

$$
\boxed{\theta=\theta_0},
\qquad 0\le \theta_0<2\pi.
$$

Therefore the curves of heat flow are rays from the origin.

To determine the direction of heat flow, note that the outer boundary has temperature

$$
100^\circ
$$

and the inner boundary has temperature

$$
0^\circ.
$$

So heat flows from the outer circle toward the inner circle. Therefore the arrows on the heat-flow curves point radially inward.

##### problem 31(d)

    ]},

 PlotLabel -> Style["Problem 31: Isotherms and Curves of Heat Flow", 18]

##### problem 32(a)

In Figure 20 the annulus is

$$
A_{1,8},
$$

so

$$
R_1=1,
\qquad
R_2=8.
$$

The boundary values are

$$
u(1,\theta)=-273.15,
\qquad
u(8,\theta)=4.
$$

Thus

$$
T_1=-273.15,
\qquad
T_2=4.
$$

Substitute these values into the formula from Exercise 30:

$$
u(r)=T_1+\left(T_2-T_1\right)\frac{\ln(r/R_1)}{\ln(R_2/R_1)}.
$$

This gives

$$
u(r)=-273.15+\left(4-(-273.15)\right)\frac{\ln(r/1)}{\ln(8/1)}.
$$

Simplify

$$
4-(-273.15)=277.15
$$

and

$$
\ln(r/1)=\ln r,
\qquad
\ln(8/1)=\ln 8,
$$

to obtain

$$
u(r)=-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln r}{\ln 8}.
$$

Therefore the solution is

$$
\boxed{u(r,\theta)=u(r)=-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln r}{\ln 8}},
\qquad 1<r<8.
$$

We check the boundary values directly. If $r=1$, then

$$
u(1,\theta)=-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln 1}{\ln 8}=-273.15+277.15\cdot 0=-273.15.
$$

If $r=8$, then

$$
u(8,\theta)=-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln 8}{\ln 8}=-273.15+277.15=4.
$$

So the formula satisfies the required boundary conditions.

##### problem 32(b)

The isotherms are the level curves of

$$
u(r)=-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln r}{\ln 8}.
$$

Set

$$
u(r)=T,
$$

where

$$
-273.15<T<4.
$$

Then

$$
-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln r}{\ln 8}=T.
$$

Add

$$
273.15
$$

to both sides:

$$
277.15\frac{\ln r}{\ln 8}=T+273.15.
$$

Multiply both sides by

$$
\frac{\ln 8}{277.15}
$$

to get

$$
\ln r=\frac{(T+273.15)\ln 8}{277.15}.
$$

Exponentiate:

$$
r=e^{((T+273.15)\ln 8)/277.15}.
$$

Use

$$
e^{a\ln 8}=8^a
$$

to obtain

$$
\boxed{r=8^{(T+273.15)/277.15}},
\qquad -273.15<T<4.
$$

Therefore the isotherms are concentric circles centered at the origin.

##### problem 32(c)

The isotherms found in part (b) are again the circles

$$
r=\text{constant}.
$$

The curves of heat flow are orthogonal to the isotherms. Since rays

$$
\theta=\theta_0
$$

meet circles centered at the origin at right angles, the heat-flow curves are rays from the origin.

Analytically, if we remove one ray from the annulus and choose a branch of $\operatorname{Log} z$ on the resulting slit annulus, then

$$
f(z)=-273.15+277.15\frac{\operatorname{Log} z}{\ln 8}
$$

has real part

$$
\Re f(z)
=-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln|z|}{\ln 8}
=-273.15+277.15\frac{\ln r}{\ln 8}
=u(r),
$$

so a local harmonic conjugate is

$$
v(r,\theta)=277.15\frac{\theta}{\ln 8},
$$

up to an additive constant. Its level curves satisfy

$$
277.15\frac{\theta}{\ln 8}=C,
$$

hence

$$
\boxed{\theta=\theta_0},
\qquad 0\le \theta_0<2\pi.
$$

Therefore the curves of heat flow are rays from the origin.

To determine the direction of heat flow, note that the outer boundary has temperature

$$
4^\circ
$$

and the inner boundary has temperature

$$
-273.15^\circ.
$$

So heat flows from the outer circle toward the inner circle. Therefore the arrows on the heat-flow curves point radially inward.
