> [!exercise] Exercise 34
> 
> 43. **Project Problem:** Laplacian in polar coordinates. In this exercise, you are asked to derive the polar form of the Laplacian
> 
> $$
> \Delta u=\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial r^{2}}+\frac{1}{r} \frac{\partial u}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}} \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta^{2}}
> $$
> 
> The derivation is straightforward but a little tedious. To organize your approach, follow the outlined steps.
> (a) Recall the relationship between rectangular and polar coordinates
> 
> $$
> x=r \cos \theta, \quad y=r \sin \theta, \quad r^{2}=x^{2}+y^{2}, \quad \tan \theta=\frac{y}{x}
> $$
> 
> Differentiate $r^{2}=x^{2}+y^{2}$ with respect to $x$ once, then a second time, and obtain
> 
> $$
> \frac{\partial r}{\partial x}=\frac{x}{r}, \quad \frac{\partial^{2} r}{\partial x^{2}}=\frac{y^{2}}{r^{3}}
> $$
> 
> (b) Differentiate $\tan \theta=\frac{y}{x}$ with respect to $x$ once, then a second time, and get
> 
> $$
> \frac{\partial \theta}{\partial x}=-\frac{y}{r^{2}}, \quad \frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial x^{2}}=\frac{2 x y}{r^{4}}
> $$
> 
> (c) In a similar way, differentiate with respect to $y$ and obtain
> 
> $$
> \frac{\partial r}{\partial y}=\frac{y}{r}, \quad \frac{\partial^{2} r}{\partial y^{2}}=\frac{x^{2}}{r^{3}}, \quad \frac{\partial \theta}{\partial y}=\frac{x}{r^{2}}, \quad \frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial y^{2}}=-\frac{2 x y}{r^{4}}
> $$
> 
> (d) From the previous identities, derive
> 
> $$
> \frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial y^{2}}=0 \quad \text { and } \quad \frac{\partial \theta}{\partial x} \frac{\partial r}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial \theta}{\partial y} \frac{\partial r}{\partial y}=0
> $$
> 
> (What does the first equation say about the function $\theta(x, y) ?$ )
> (e) Use the chain rule in two dimensions ((13), Section 2.6) to derive
> 
> $$
> \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial x^{2}}=\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial r^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial r}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+2 \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta \partial r} \frac{\partial r}{\partial x} \frac{\partial \theta}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial u}{\partial r} \frac{\partial^{2} r}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial \theta}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+\frac{\partial u}{\partial \theta} \frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial x^{2}}
> $$
> 
> Change $x$ to $y$ and obtain
> 
> $$
> \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial y^{2}}=\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial r^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial r}{\partial y}\right)^{2}+2 \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta \partial r} \frac{\partial r}{\partial y} \frac{\partial \theta}{\partial y}+\frac{\partial u}{\partial r} \frac{\partial^{2} r}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial \theta}{\partial y}\right)^{2}+\frac{\partial u}{\partial \theta} \frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial y^{2}}
> $$
> 
> (f) Add $\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial x^{2}}$ and $\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial y^{2}}$ and simplify with the help of (d) to derive (12).



##### solution

Throughout this exercise we work with

$$
r>0.
$$

For brevity, write

$$
r_x=\frac{\partial r}{\partial x},
\qquad
r_y=\frac{\partial r}{\partial y},
\qquad
\theta_x=\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial x},
\qquad
\theta_y=\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial y},
$$

and similarly for second derivatives.

##### problem 43(a)

$$
\begin{aligned}
r^2=x^2+y^2
&\implies
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(r^2)
=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2+y^2)\\
&\implies
2rr_x=2x\\
&\implies
r_x=\frac{x}{r}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
r_{xx}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{x}{r}\right)\\
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(xr^{-1})\\
&=
\frac{\partial x}{\partial x}\,r^{-1}
+
x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(r^{-1})\\
&=
\frac{1}{r}
+
x(-r^{-2})r_x\\
&=
\frac{1}{r}-xr^{-2}\frac{x}{r}\\
&=
\frac{1}{r}-\frac{x^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{r^2}{r^3}-\frac{x^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{r^2-x^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{x^2+y^2-x^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{y^2}{r^3}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{
\frac{\partial r}{\partial x}=\frac{x}{r},
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2 r}{\partial x^2}=\frac{y^2}{r^3}.
}
$$

##### problem 43(b)

$$
\begin{aligned}
\tan\theta=\frac{y}{x}
&\implies
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(\tan\theta)
=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\\
&\implies
\sec^2\theta\,\theta_x
=
y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^{-1})\\
&\implies
\sec^2\theta\,\theta_x
=
y(-x^{-2})\\
&\implies
\sec^2\theta\,\theta_x
=
-\frac{y}{x^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\sec^2\theta
&=
1+\tan^2\theta\\
&=
1+\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^2\\
&=
1+\frac{y^2}{x^2}\\
&=
\frac{x^2+y^2}{x^2}\\
&=
\frac{r^2}{x^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{r^2}{x^2}\theta_x
&=
-\frac{y}{x^2}\\
\theta_x
&=
-\frac{y}{x^2}\cdot\frac{x^2}{r^2}\\
&=
-\frac{y}{r^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\theta_{xx}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(-\frac{y}{r^2}\right)\\
&=
-y\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(r^{-2})\\
&=
-y(-2r^{-3}r_x)\\
&=
2yr^{-3}\frac{x}{r}\\
&=
\frac{2xy}{r^4}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{
\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial x}=-\frac{y}{r^2},
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2\theta}{\partial x^2}=\frac{2xy}{r^4}.
}
$$

##### problem 43(c)

$$
\begin{aligned}
r^2=x^2+y^2
&\implies
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(r^2)
=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2+y^2)\\
&\implies
2rr_y=2y\\
&\implies
r_y=\frac{y}{r}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
r_{yy}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{y}{r}\right)\\
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(yr^{-1})\\
&=
\frac{\partial y}{\partial y}\,r^{-1}
+
y\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(r^{-1})\\
&=
\frac{1}{r}
+
y(-r^{-2})r_y\\
&=
\frac{1}{r}-yr^{-2}\frac{y}{r}\\
&=
\frac{1}{r}-\frac{y^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{r^2}{r^3}-\frac{y^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{r^2-y^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{x^2+y^2-y^2}{r^3}\\
&=
\frac{x^2}{r^3}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\tan\theta=\frac{y}{x}
&\implies
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(\tan\theta)
=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)\\
&\implies
\sec^2\theta\,\theta_y
=
\frac{1}{x}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{r^2}{x^2}\theta_y
&=
\frac{1}{x}\\
\theta_y
&=
\frac{1}{x}\cdot\frac{x^2}{r^2}\\
&=
\frac{x}{r^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\theta_{yy}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{x}{r^2}\right)\\
&=
x\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(r^{-2})\\
&=
x(-2r^{-3}r_y)\\
&=
x\left(-2r^{-3}\frac{y}{r}\right)\\
&=
-\frac{2xy}{r^4}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{
\frac{\partial r}{\partial y}=\frac{y}{r},
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2 r}{\partial y^2}=\frac{x^2}{r^3},
\qquad
\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial y}=\frac{x}{r^2},
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2\theta}{\partial y^2}=-\frac{2xy}{r^4}.
}
$$

##### problem 43(d)

$$
\theta_{xx}+\theta_{yy}
=
\frac{2xy}{r^4}+\left(-\frac{2xy}{r^4}\right)
=
0.
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\theta_x r_x+\theta_y r_y
&=
\left(-\frac{y}{r^2}\right)\left(\frac{x}{r}\right)
+
\left(\frac{x}{r^2}\right)\left(\frac{y}{r}\right)\\
&=
-\frac{xy}{r^3}+\frac{xy}{r^3}\\
&=
0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{
\frac{\partial^2\theta}{\partial x^2}+\frac{\partial^2\theta}{\partial y^2}=0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial x}\frac{\partial r}{\partial x}
+\frac{\partial\theta}{\partial y}\frac{\partial r}{\partial y}=0.
}
$$

The first identity says that

$$
\theta(x,y)
$$

is harmonic wherever it is single-valued and twice differentiable.

##### problem 43(e)

Write

$$
u_r=\frac{\partial u}{\partial r},
\qquad
u_\theta=\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta},
\qquad
u_{rr}=\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial r^2},
\qquad
u_{r\theta}=\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial r\partial\theta},
\qquad
u_{\theta\theta}=\frac{\partial^2 u}{\partial \theta^2}.
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_x
&=
u_r r_x+u_\theta\theta_x,\\
u_{xx}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u_r r_x+u_\theta\theta_x)\\
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u_r r_x)
+
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(u_\theta\theta_x)\\
&=
(u_r)_x r_x+u_r r_{xx}+(u_\theta)_x\theta_x+u_\theta\theta_{xx}\\
&=
(u_{rr}r_x+u_{r\theta}\theta_x)r_x+u_r r_{xx}
+
(u_{\theta r}r_x+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_x)\theta_x+u_\theta\theta_{xx}\\
&=
u_{rr}r_x^2+u_{r\theta}\theta_xr_x+u_r r_{xx}
+
u_{\theta r}r_x\theta_x+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_x^2+u_\theta\theta_{xx}\\
&=
u_{rr}r_x^2+2u_{r\theta}r_x\theta_x+u_r r_{xx}+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_x^2+u_\theta\theta_{xx}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Similarly,

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_y
&=
u_r r_y+u_\theta\theta_y,\\
u_{yy}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(u_r r_y+u_\theta\theta_y)\\
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(u_r r_y)
+
\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(u_\theta\theta_y)\\
&=
(u_r)_y r_y+u_r r_{yy}+(u_\theta)_y\theta_y+u_\theta\theta_{yy}\\
&=
(u_{rr}r_y+u_{r\theta}\theta_y)r_y+u_r r_{yy}
+
(u_{\theta r}r_y+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_y)\theta_y+u_\theta\theta_{yy}\\
&=
u_{rr}r_y^2+u_{r\theta}\theta_yr_y+u_r r_{yy}
+
u_{\theta r}r_y\theta_y+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_y^2+u_\theta\theta_{yy}\\
&=
u_{rr}r_y^2+2u_{r\theta}r_y\theta_y+u_r r_{yy}+u_{\theta\theta}\theta_y^2+u_\theta\theta_{yy}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{
\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial x^{2}}
=
\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial r^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial r}{\partial x}\right)^{2}
+2 \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta \partial r} \frac{\partial r}{\partial x} \frac{\partial \theta}{\partial x}
+\frac{\partial u}{\partial r} \frac{\partial^{2} r}{\partial x^{2}}
+\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial \theta}{\partial x}\right)^{2}
+\frac{\partial u}{\partial \theta} \frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial x^{2}}.
}
$$

$$
\boxed{
\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial y^{2}}
=
\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial r^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial r}{\partial y}\right)^{2}
+2 \frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta \partial r} \frac{\partial r}{\partial y} \frac{\partial \theta}{\partial y}
+\frac{\partial u}{\partial r} \frac{\partial^{2} r}{\partial y^{2}}
+\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial \theta}{\partial y}\right)^{2}
+\frac{\partial u}{\partial \theta} \frac{\partial^{2} \theta}{\partial y^{2}}.
}
$$

##### problem 43(f)

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta u
&=
u_{xx}+u_{yy}\\
&=
u_{rr}(r_x^2+r_y^2)
+2u_{r\theta}(r_x\theta_x+r_y\theta_y)
+u_r(r_{xx}+r_{yy})
+u_{\theta\theta}(\theta_x^2+\theta_y^2)
+u_\theta(\theta_{xx}+\theta_{yy})\\
&=
u_{rr}\left[\left(\frac{x}{r}\right)^2+\left(\frac{y}{r}\right)^2\right]
+2u_{r\theta}\left[\left(\frac{x}{r}\right)\left(-\frac{y}{r^2}\right)+\left(\frac{y}{r}\right)\left(\frac{x}{r^2}\right)\right]\\
&\qquad
+u_r\left(\frac{y^2}{r^3}+\frac{x^2}{r^3}\right)
+u_{\theta\theta}\left[\left(-\frac{y}{r^2}\right)^2+\left(\frac{x}{r^2}\right)^2\right]
+u_\theta\left(\frac{2xy}{r^4}-\frac{2xy}{r^4}\right)\\
&=
u_{rr}\left(\frac{x^2+y^2}{r^2}\right)
+2u_{r\theta}\left(-\frac{xy}{r^3}+\frac{xy}{r^3}\right)
+u_r\left(\frac{x^2+y^2}{r^3}\right)
+u_{\theta\theta}\left(\frac{y^2+x^2}{r^4}\right)
+u_\theta(0)\\
&=
u_{rr}\left(\frac{r^2}{r^2}\right)
+2u_{r\theta}(0)
+u_r\left(\frac{r^2}{r^3}\right)
+u_{\theta\theta}\left(\frac{r^2}{r^4}\right)\\
&=
u_{rr}
+\frac{1}{r}u_r
+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\boxed{
\Delta u
=
\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial r^{2}}
+\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial u}{\partial r}
+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2} u}{\partial \theta^{2}}.
}
$$



++++




> [!exercise] Exercise 35
> 44. (a) Use (12) to give a direct proof of the result of problem 17(a).
> (b) Use (12) to give a direct proof that $\log |z|$ is harmonic for all $z \neq 0$.



##### solution

##### problem 44(a)

Let

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
$$

and

$$
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta),
$$

where $n$ is any integer.

We use the polar form of the Laplacian:

$$
\Delta w
=
\frac{\partial^2 w}{\partial r^2}
+\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial w}{\partial r}
+\frac{1}{r^2}\frac{\partial^2 w}{\partial \theta^2}.
$$

We first compute the Laplacian of

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta).
$$

Its $r$-derivatives are

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_r
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r^n\cos(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
\cos(n\theta)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^n)\\
&=
\cos(n\theta)\,nr^{n-1}\\
&=
nr^{n-1}\cos(n\theta),
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{rr}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(nr^{n-1}\cos(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
n\cos(n\theta)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r^{n-1}\right)\\
&=
n\cos(n\theta)(n-1)r^{n-2}\\
&=
n(n-1)r^{n-2}\cos(n\theta).
\end{aligned}
$$

Its $\theta$-derivatives are

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_\theta
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(r^n\cos(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
r^n\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(\cos(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
r^n\bigl(-\sin(n\theta)\bigr)n\\
&=
-nr^n\sin(n\theta),
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{\theta\theta}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(-nr^n\sin(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
-nr^n\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(\sin(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
-nr^n\bigl(\cos(n\theta)\bigr)n\\
&=
-n^2r^n\cos(n\theta).
\end{aligned}
$$

Substitute these into the polar Laplacian:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta u
&=
u_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}u_r+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}\\
&=
n(n-1)r^{n-2}\cos(n\theta)
+\frac{1}{r}\left(nr^{n-1}\cos(n\theta)\right)
+\frac{1}{r^2}\left(-n^2r^n\cos(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
n(n-1)r^{n-2}\cos(n\theta)
+nr^{n-2}\cos(n\theta)
-n^2r^{n-2}\cos(n\theta)\\
&=
\bigl(n(n-1)+n-n^2\bigr)r^{n-2}\cos(n\theta)\\
&=
\bigl(n^2-n+n-n^2\bigr)r^{n-2}\cos(n\theta)\\
&=
0
\end{aligned}
$$

for

$$
r>0.
$$

So

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^n\cos(n\theta)
$$

is harmonic on

$$
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}.
$$

Now do the same for

$$
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta).
$$

Its $r$-derivatives are

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_r
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r^n\sin(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
\sin(n\theta)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^n)\\
&=
\sin(n\theta)\,nr^{n-1}\\
&=
nr^{n-1}\sin(n\theta),
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_{rr}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(nr^{n-1}\sin(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
n\sin(n\theta)\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r^{n-1}\right)\\
&=
n\sin(n\theta)(n-1)r^{n-2}\\
&=
n(n-1)r^{n-2}\sin(n\theta).
\end{aligned}
$$

Its $\theta$-derivatives are

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_\theta
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(r^n\sin(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
r^n\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(\sin(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
r^n\bigl(\cos(n\theta)\bigr)n\\
&=
nr^n\cos(n\theta),
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
v_{\theta\theta}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(nr^n\cos(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
nr^n\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}\left(\cos(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
nr^n\bigl(-\sin(n\theta)\bigr)n\\
&=
-n^2r^n\sin(n\theta).
\end{aligned}
$$

Substitute into the polar Laplacian:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta v
&=
v_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}v_r+\frac{1}{r^2}v_{\theta\theta}\\
&=
n(n-1)r^{n-2}\sin(n\theta)
+\frac{1}{r}\left(nr^{n-1}\sin(n\theta)\right)
+\frac{1}{r^2}\left(-n^2r^n\sin(n\theta)\right)\\
&=
n(n-1)r^{n-2}\sin(n\theta)
+nr^{n-2}\sin(n\theta)
-n^2r^{n-2}\sin(n\theta)\\
&=
\bigl(n(n-1)+n-n^2\bigr)r^{n-2}\sin(n\theta)\\
&=
\bigl(n^2-n+n-n^2\bigr)r^{n-2}\sin(n\theta)\\
&=
0
\end{aligned}
$$

for

$$
r>0.
$$

So

$$
v(r,\theta)=r^n\sin(n\theta)
$$

is also harmonic on

$$
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}.
$$

This already proves the result for

$$
n<0,
$$

because when $n<0$ the factor

$$
r^n
$$

is not defined at

$$
r=0.
$$

Now suppose

$$
n\ge 0.
$$

If

$$
n=0,
$$

then

$$
u(r,\theta)=r^0\cos 0=1,
\qquad
v(r,\theta)=r^0\sin 0=0,
$$

and both functions are harmonic on all of

$$
\mathbb{C}.
$$

If

$$
n\ge 1,
$$

then

$$
z^n=(x+iy)^n
$$

is a polynomial in

$$
x
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
y.
$$

Its real and imaginary parts are therefore polynomials in

$$
x
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
y,
$$

and those real and imaginary parts are exactly

$$
r^n\cos(n\theta)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
r^n\sin(n\theta).
$$

Hence these functions extend across

$$
r=0
$$

without any singularity, so together with the computation above they are harmonic on all of

$$
\mathbb{C}.
$$

Therefore

$$
\boxed{
u(r,\theta)=r^{n}\cos(n\theta)
\quad\text{and}\quad
v(r,\theta)=r^{n}\sin(n\theta)
}
$$

are harmonic on

$$
\mathbb{C}
\quad\text{if }n\ge 0,
$$

and on

$$
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
\quad\text{if }n<0.
$$

##### problem 44(b)

Let

$$
u(x,y)=\log|z|.
$$

In polar coordinates,

$$
|z|=r,
$$

so

$$
u(r,\theta)=\log r.
$$

Now compute its derivatives.

Because

$$
u(r,\theta)=\log r,
$$

we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_r
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(\log r)\\
&=
\frac{1}{r},
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{rr}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)\\
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^{-1})\\
&=
-r^{-2}\\
&=
-\frac{1}{r^2}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Since

$$
\log r
$$

does not depend on

$$
\theta,
$$

we also have

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_\theta
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}(\log r)\\
&=
0,
\end{aligned}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{\theta\theta}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial \theta}(0)\\
&=
0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now substitute into the polar form of the Laplacian:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Delta u
&=
u_{rr}+\frac{1}{r}u_r+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}\\
&=
\left(-\frac{1}{r^2}\right)
+\frac{1}{r}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)
+\frac{1}{r^2}(0)\\
&=
-\frac{1}{r^2}+\frac{1}{r^2}+0\\
&=
0
\end{aligned}
$$

for

$$
r>0.
$$

Since

$$
r>0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
z\neq 0,
$$

we conclude that

$$
\boxed{
\log|z|
}
$$

is harmonic for all

$$
z\neq 0.
$$



++++



> [!exercise] Exercise 36
> 45. Show that if $u$ is harmonic and independent of $r$, then $u_{\theta \theta}=0$. Conclude that $u=a \theta+b$; equivalently, $u=a \arg _{\alpha} z+b$, where $\arg _{\alpha} z$ is a branch of the argument.



##### solution

Assume that

$$
u=u(r,\theta)
$$

is harmonic and independent of

$$
r.
$$

To say that $u$ is independent of $r$ means that there is a function of $\theta$ alone, say

$$
\phi(\theta),
$$

such that

$$
u(r,\theta)=\phi(\theta).
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_r
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\phi(\theta)\\
&=
0,
\end{aligned}
$$

and then

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{rr}
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(u_r)\\
&=
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(0)\\
&=
0.
\end{aligned}
$$

Now use the polar form of the Laplacian:

$$
\Delta u
=
u_{rr}
+\frac{1}{r}u_r
+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}.
$$

Since $u$ is harmonic,

$$
\Delta u=0.
$$

Substitute the formulas

$$
u_{rr}=0
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
u_r=0:
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
0
&=
u_{rr}
+\frac{1}{r}u_r
+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}\\
&=
0
+\frac{1}{r}(0)
+\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}\\
&=
\frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Multiply both sides by

$$
r^2
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
r^2\cdot 0
&=
r^2\cdot \frac{1}{r^2}u_{\theta\theta}\\
0
&=
u_{\theta\theta}.
\end{aligned}
$$

Thus

$$
\boxed{
u_{\theta\theta}=0.
}
$$

We now solve this differential equation in $\theta$.

From

$$
u_{\theta\theta}=0,
$$

integrate once with respect to $\theta$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u_{\theta}
&=
\int 0\,d\theta\\
&=
a,
\end{aligned}
$$

where $a$ is a real constant.

Integrate again with respect to $\theta$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
u
&=
\int a\,d\theta\\
&=
a\theta+b,
\end{aligned}
$$

where $b$ is another real constant.

Therefore

$$
\boxed{
u=a\theta+b.
}
$$

Finally, on any domain where a branch of the argument is defined, we have

$$
\theta=\arg_{\alpha} z.
$$

So the same formula may be written as

$$
\boxed{
u=a\arg_{\alpha} z+b.
}
$$


++++
